Add the edges where routing applies.
Interoperate between the top-level graph state and the state of the research
sub-graphs.
Reuse the enter/exit functions to wrap the graph.
With the graph constructed, you can execute it in streaming mode. The call in
Example 9-5 sends a single high-level prompt to the supervisor and lets the team iter‐
ate until completion. Streaming yields incremental state updates from nodes as
they act, which you can print for visibility while skipping the final event. The
recursion_limit bounds the number of agent steps to protect against runaway loops:
situations where agents keep passing control back and forth without ever reaching a
finishing condition. For example, you might have a runaway loop if the supervisor
repeatedly reassigns tasks or a worker keeps requesting retries indefinitely. The
prompt instructs the team to search, collect sources and patent links, and then write
the final report to disk as a .txt file.
Example 9-5. Run the supervisor architecture
for s in authoring_chain.stream(
"""Write an 800-word-long research report white paper on semiconductor
development which includes an executive summary at the beginning about
your findings. Search also for relevant recent patents and include the
links to it. IMPORTANT: Provide the links to all your sources, and then
write the report to disk as .txt file.""",
{"recursion_limit": 100},
):
if "__end__" not in s:
print(s)
print("---")
By combining specialized agents, explicit routing, and state machine control this way,
you enable AI systems to handle tasks that would overwhelm a single agent. This
approach not only improves accuracy and efficiency through domain specialization
but also provides a clear, expandable and maintainable structure for complex work‐
flows. Whether you apply it to research, analysis, or planning, this design gives you
fine-grained control over your execution flow while agents remain flexible enough to
adapt their actions dynamically.
254 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
Agentic Communication: The Right
Context Is All You Need
This section gives you guidance about why more information does not always mean
better answers in agentic systems. Just as personas shape how an agent performs and
responds, context determines how effectively it can carry out a task. Even the most
capable human can’t perform their job well without first understanding the context of
what they are being asked to do, and the same is true for AI agents. The term prompt
engineering describes the craft of writing instructions in the optimal format for an
LLM chatbot. Context engineering takes this further by dynamically generating and
adapting that context within an agentic system. This requires a deeper understanding
of both the task and the system’s evolving state, making it one of the most important
responsibilities for anyone building AI agents. In practice, this means designing sys‐
tems that can recognize, assemble, and refine the information an agent needs before it
acts.
To understand why this matters, imagine asking an AI travel assistant, “Can you plan
my next holiday?” A basic prototype might see only that one question and respond
with something like, “Sure, where would you like to go?” The agent might run
without errors, but without richer context, the answer is generic and forces the user
to repeat information they’ve already provided in the past.
Now consider the same request in an agent designed with full context engineering.
Before responding, the agent recalls the user’s travel history from previous interac‐
tions, including preferred destinations, budget ranges, and typical travel times. It
retrieves loyalty program details and checks the user’s calendar for available dates. It
then uses a live search tool to gather current news and weather conditions and finds
that a hurricane is forecast to hit the user’s usual vacation region next month. With
this richer picture, the agent can reply:
I remember you usually spend early September in the Caribbean, but there is a
hurricane warning for that period. Based on your past trips and budget, I found
a similar beachfront resort in Madeira with availability for the same dates.
It’s part of your hotel loyalty program and has excellent reviews. Would you
like me to book the reservation?
The difference between the two agents is not in the complexity of the model but in
the completeness and relevance of the context. Prompt engineering focuses on craft‐
ing a single well-formed instruction, while context engineering is about building sys‐
tems that dynamically assemble the right mix of historical knowledge, real-time
information, and available tools so that the agent can act with precision and foresight.
To make context engineering tangible, it helps to look at the key components an agent
can draw on when shaping its response. These elements work together inside the
broader context space, each influencing how the agent interprets the user’s request
Agentic Communication: The Right Context Is All You Need | 255
and decides on the best course of action. As shown in Figure 9-5, the user prompt sits
at the center, with all other components surrounding and informing it.
Figure 9-5. Context engineering components centered on the user prompt. Each element
contributes to shaping the agent’s understanding and response within the overall
context space.
Below are short explanations for what each context engineering component entails:
Instructions/system prompt
The foundational guide that shapes how the model behaves throughout the con‐
versation. This can include explicit rules, constraints, style preferences, and
worked examples. Well-crafted system prompts align the model’s responses with
the agent’s intended persona and objectives.
User prompt
The immediate request or task provided by the user. It’s the focal point around
which all other context elements revolve, ensuring that the system’s output
directly addresses the user’s needs.
State/history (short-term memory)
The running record of the current conversation, including both user inputs and
model outputs. This temporary memory enables continuity, prevents repetition,
and maintains coherence over multi-turn interactions.
256 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
Long-term memory
A persistent knowledge store accumulated over multiple sessions. It can contain
user preferences, summaries of past projects, recurring workflows, or other facts
the agent has been explicitly instructed to remember for future use.
Retrieved information (RAG)
External, up-to-date knowledge pulled from documents, databases, APIs, or the
web at the time of the query. This retrieval step enriches the agent’s knowledge
base with context that is specific, timely, and beyond its static training data.
Available tools
The set of functions, APIs, or capabilities the agent can invoke to take action.
Examples range from checking inventory and sending emails to running data
analyses or booking reservations. This can also include integrations built on the
Model Context Protocol (MCP), which provides a standardized way for agents to
access external systems and services. The toolset determines what actions are
possible in response to the user’s prompt.
Structured output
Explicit specifications for how the model should return its answer, such as a
JSON object, HTML snippet, or table. Structured formats are essential when out‐
puts must be machine-readable or integrated into downstream workflows.
Understanding these components is only the first step. Effective context engineering
also involves deciding how and when each element is brought into the model’s active
reasoning space. A common pitfall when building agentic systems is to provide every
possible piece of context in every interaction. This can quickly overwhelm the mod‐
el’s context window and dilute the relevance of the information that actually matters
for the task. Effective context engineering is therefore as much about restraint as it is
about completeness. The goal is to present the model with only the most useful and
targeted information at the right moment.
This process begins with selecting the most relevant information for the task at hand.
A well-designed agent does not simply load all possible context at once. Instead, it
identifies what’s needed for the current request, which may involve retrieving relevant
memories, narrowing the available toolset to avoid confusion, or pulling the most rel‐
evant knowledge from external sources. The aim is to ensure that the user prompt is
surrounded by information that’s timely, accurate, and directly related to the question
being asked.
Once relevant context is selected, it must often be compressed to fit within the mod‐
el’s available context window. Long conversations, detailed tool outputs, or extensive
retrieval results can quickly exceed capacity or lead to the haystack problem covered
in Chapter 1. Summarization offers one way to retain the essence of this information
while removing redundancy and can be applied in stages to preserve important
Agentic Communication: The Right Context Is All You Need | 257
details across many turns. Trimming uses a more selective approach, removing less
relevant content according to fixed rules or trained filtering mechanisms. Both
approaches aim to give the model as much useful input as possible without over‐
whelming it with unnecessary detail.
There are also cases where it’s more effective to isolate context entirely. In MASs, each
agent may have its own dedicated instructions, memory, and tools, focusing only on a
specific part of the task. This separation allows each agent to work without interfer‐
ence from unrelated information while still contributing to a shared goal through
structured communication and coordination.
In practice, context engineering is therefore as much about management as it is about
collection. Selecting, compressing, and isolating information ensures that when the
model interprets the user’s request, it does so within a context that is both rich and
precisely tuned to the task at hand. This balance is what allows an agent to act with
accuracy, efficiency, and adaptability.
Beyond Context: How to Help Agents Remember
Context engineering determines what an agent knows at the moment of decision.
Memory determines how that knowledge is retained, organized, and recalled over
time. To make this concrete, it helps to start with the basic memory categories that
underlie both human cognition and agent system design.
Agent Memory Types
In cognitive science, memory is often grouped into three broad types, which we can
directly map to memory design in agents. Figure 9-6 shows an overview of these
types in the context of coding agents.
Figure 9-6. Overview of different memory types.
258 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
These categories are practical for agents because they help clarify what information is
stored and how it’s used. A coding agent, for example, may rely on procedural mem‐
ory for its own internal code and the LLM it runs on, episodic memory for recalling
relevant code snippets or examples from past work, and semantic memory for
retrieving documentation or project details. The categories include:
Procedural memory
“How-to” knowledge. This encodes skills, workflows, and action patterns. For
agents, procedural memory might include learned tool invocation sequences,
optimized query strategies, or a preferred order of reasoning steps. It’s often
updated incrementally through fine-tuning or reinforcement learning and
retrieved automatically during execution.
Episodic memory
Memory of specific events or interactions. In an AI agent, this could be the log of
a past troubleshooting session, the sequence of actions taken in a customer sup‐
port case, or the steps followed in a successful trade execution. Episodic memory
supports reflection, replay, and postmortem analysis of task outcomes.
Semantic memory
Factual and conceptual knowledge, independent of the event in which it was
learned. This might be a vector database of patents, a curated financial knowledge
base, or embeddings of technical documentation. Semantic memory can be
searched and combined with current observations to answer complex questions.
These categories are not mutually exclusive. A single workflow can draw on all three:
procedural memory to know how to act, episodic memory to recall when a similar
case happened, and semantic memory to fetch what facts are relevant. In practice,
these types of memory are not just about what’s stored but also about how widely that
information can be shared and how long it’s kept. This is where memory scope comes
in, moving us from the question of what an agent remembers to how long and with
whom it remembers it.
Going Global and Lifelong
In a single agent setup, all memory is local. It lives with that agent alone. Local mem‐
ory lets the agent specialize and keep its own notes without anyone else looking over
its shoulder. In MAS, you can go wider. A global or shared memory acts like a com‐
mon whiteboard all agents can see and write on. It’s where knowledge can flow
without the agents having to send direct messages to one another. Then there’s lifelong
memory, which sticks around across sessions and projects, letting the agent or the
whole team build up experience over time instead of starting from scratch every
morning.
Beyond Context: How to Help Agents Remember | 259
Letta is a stateful agents framework providing lifelong memory, reasoning, and con‐
text management. It treats an agent’s context window like the RAM in a computer,
fast but limited, and then adds an external persistent store that works like a hard
drive. When the agent’s working context starts to fill up, Letta automatically moves
less relevant information out to long-term storage and can pull it back later if needed.
This allows the agent to operate as if it has an almost unlimited memory, even when it
runs on a model with a fixed context size. Example 9-6 shows how to create a shared
memory block and attach it to a supervisor agent and a worker agent. This is an
example from Letta’s API documentation.
Example 9-6. Create a shared memory block
client = Letta(base_url="http://127.0.0.1:8283")
shared_block = client.blocks.create(label="organization",
value="Organization: Letta")
supervisor_agent = client.agents.create(
model="anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022",
embedding="openai/text-embedding-3-small",
# blocks created for this agentj
memory_blocks=[{"label": "persona", "value": "I am a supervisor"}],
# pre-existing shared block that is "attached" to this agent
block_ids=[shared_block.id],
)
worker_agent = client.agents.create(
model="anthropic/claude-3-5-sonnet-20241022",
embedding="openai/text-embedding-3-small",
# blocks created for this agent
memory_blocks=[{"label": "persona", "value": "I am a worker"}],
# pre-existing shared block that is "attached" to this agent
block_ids=[shared_block.id],
)
Create a shared memory block.
Create a supervisor agent.
Create a worker agent.
The important part is that the agent manages this process itself. It decides what to
keep immediately available, what to archive, and when to go searching through past
records. In practice, this means an agent built with Letta can remember details from
conversations weeks ago, reference older documents while working on a new task, or
260 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
keep track of evolving strategies without a human having to re-upload the same back‐
ground material again and again.
With Letta, you can keep a fast and finite local memory for active reasoning, a larger
shared or persistent memory for the information you might need later, and a smart
way of moving information between them. Whether you’re running a single agent
that needs to remember its entire history or a group of agents that share one common
knowledge base, the result is the same. Agents work better together when they aren’t
forced to operate with goldfish memory.
The Human Factor: Steering Agent Actions
As you saw in “Autonomy: What’s Possible at the Moment?” on page 244, agents have
a certain amount of autonomy in how they carry out tasks. But even with that limited
freedom, you might be wondering, “Can I really trust an agent to act the right way
without me watching?” The answer is…not always. You can put safeguards in place,
like a judge or verifier LLM, and you can test how accurate its outputs are. But some‐
times agents still misread a prompt or take an action they shouldn’t. And if that
action affects something sensitive, like access control, financial operations, or cus‐
tomer data, that isn’t a risk worth taking. This is where a human-in-the-loop (HITL)
setup makes all the difference, giving the agent a chance to pause and get your appro‐
val before making an important move.
Inserting humans’ approval at key decision points is not just about controlling the
agent. It’s about protecting you and your organization from costly mistakes. With a
HITL protocol, every critical action passes through a human reviewer or approver
before it’s executed. This ensures that irreversible errors are caught in time, that there
is clear accountability for every decision, and that you remain compliant with audit
requirements such as SOC 2 policies and internal governance rules. It also turns your
AI from a black box into a supervised assistant, building trust with both users and
stakeholders. Therefore, this isn’t a nice-to-have. In many agentic workflows, espe‐
cially those touching high stakes operations, HITL is the only responsible path.
Common Patterns for Human-in-the-Loop
Once you decide to integrate HITL into your workflow, the question becomes how to
design those decision points so they fit naturally into your agent’s flow. The approach
you choose will depend on the type of task, the sensitivity of the action, and the
degree of oversight you want to maintain.
The Human Factor: Steering Agent Actions | 261
In LangGraph, the HITL designs are built on a single core idea: pause the graph at the
right moment and resume it only after a human provides input. LangGraph’s
interrupt() function is the key to doing this. When called inside a node, it stops
execution and saves the graph state. The human can then review or edit whatever you
surface, and you resume execution by passing the input back in using the Command
primitive.
To help you to see how the pieces fit together, I’ll walk you through a LangGraph
workflow that generates LinkedIn posts using an LLM, with an HITL feedback mech‐
anism. The flow includes three main components:
Model node
Uses a language model (Llama 3.3 via Nebius) to generate a LinkedIn post based
on a user-provided topic and optional feedback.
Human node
Pauses execution to collect user feedback on the generated post. The user can
iteratively refine the output or use a predefined keyword to finalize it.
End node
Outputs the final post and feedback summary.
The code uses LangGraph’s interrupt() and command() mechanism to handle real-
time input and supports continuous, feedback-driven improvement of the generated
content. The flow is structured using a StateGraph, and all interactions are managed
with a while True loop that handles streaming and user input.
You start with the state as shown in Example 9-7. Here, you define exactly what the
graph carries from node to node. In this example, the state holds the LinkedIn topic,
a running list of generated drafts, and a running list of human feedback entries. The
lists let you keep a full trail rather than overwriting values.
Example 9-7. Set state for HITL loop
class State(TypedDict):
linkedin_topic: str
generated_post: Annotated[List[str], add_messages]
human_feedback: Annotated[List[str], add_messages]
Topic to write about. Immutable across iterations.
List of drafts across the loop so you can inspect the full trail later.
List of feedback messages to condition future drafts.
262 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
Next comes the model node (Example 9-8). It reads the current topic and the most
recent human feedback and then drafts the next version of the post. The prompt
includes both the topic and the last piece of feedback, so each iteration can refine the
text. The node returns the new draft and passes the feedback list through unchanged.
Example 9-8. Set the model state
def model(state: State) -> State:
"""Draft a LinkedIn post using the topic and the most
recent human feedback."""
topic = state["linkedin_topic"]
feedback_list = state.get("human_feedback", [])
feedback_summary = _summarize_feedback(feedback_list)
prompt = f"""
You are an expert LinkedIn content writer. Create a strong post that:
- Opens with a clear hook in 1 to 2 short sentences
- Uses short paragraphs with concrete points and a practical example
- Avoids fluff and clichés
- Ends with a simple call to action
- Keeps a professional but conversational tone
Topic: {topic}
Latest feedback to consider: {feedback_summary}
Return plain text only.
""".strip()
response = llm.invoke([
SystemMessage(content="Write crisp, useful LinkedIn
posts with substance and clarity."),
HumanMessage(content=prompt)
])
generated = response.content
print("\n[model] Draft ready. Handing over for review...\n")
print("----- DRAFT START -----")
print(generated)
print("------ DRAFT END ------\n")
return {
"linkedin_topic": topic,
"generated_post": [AIMessage(content=generated)],
"human_feedback": feedback_list,
}
Read invariant topic from state.
Carry the existing feedback list forward.

Reduce many feedback items to a concise summary for the next prompt.
Build a tight instruction that embeds topic and feedback summary.

classes are available.
Extract generated text from the model response.
Print to console to aid review during the loop.
Pass the topic through unchanged to preserve state.
Append the latest draft as an AIMessage so downstream nodes can access content
consistently.
Pass feedback through unchanged here. The human node will update it.
Then you need to set up the human intervention node (Example 9-9). This is where
you pause execution. The node calls interrupt(...) and surfaces the current draft
along with a message that asks for feedback. The graph saves its state and stops.
When you resume, the node receives the human’s input as the return value of
interrupt(...). If the input indicates you’re done, the node routes to the end.
Otherwise, it appends the feedback and loops back to the model for another draft.
Example 9-9. Set up human node
def human_node(state: State) -> Command:
"""Pause for human feedback. Default to 'okay' if the user presses Enter."""
latest = state["generated_post"][-1].content if
state["generated_post"] else ""
print("[human_node] Review the draft above.")
print("Tip: press Enter for 'okay'. Type 'done' to finish.
Or write feedback to iterate.\n")
user_feedback = interrupt({
"generated_post": latest,
"message": "Your feedback [okay/done/custom]:"
})
feedback_list = state.get("human_feedback", [])
if isinstance(user_feedback, str):
raw = user_feedback.strip()
if not raw:
raw = "okay"
low = raw.lower()
264 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
if low in DONE_SET:
return Command(
update={"human_feedback": feedback_list + ["Finalized"]},
goto="end_node"
)
if low in OKAY_SET:
feedback_list = feedback_list + ["okay"]
else:
feedback_list = feedback_list + [raw]
else:
feedback_list = feedback_list + [str(user_feedback)]
return Command(update={"human_feedback": feedback_list}, goto="model")
Grab the most recent draft text so the reviewer sees the right content.
Signal interrupt to pause the graph and persist state. Use the return value as
human input on resume.
Copy the current feedback list so new input can be appended immutably.
Normalize user input. Map empty input to the default “okay.”
If the user says “done,” route to the terminal node and mark the list as finalized.
If the user says “okay,” record a lightweight approval token. Otherwise, record
free-form feedback.
Handle non-string inputs by converting everything to a string entry in the audit
trail.
Continue the loop by routing back to the model node with updated feedback.
To make pausing work, you need to compile the graph with a checkpointer and run
it with a thread id, as shown in Example 9-10. When an interrupt occurs, you’ll see
the special __interrupt__ marker in the stream. Resume by passing the human input
through Command(resume=...).
Example 9-10. Enable interrupt mechanism
checkpointer = MemorySaver()
app = graph.compile(checkpointer=checkpointer)
thread_config = {"configurable": {
"thread_id": uuid.uuid4()
The Human Factor: Steering Agent Actions | 265
}}
linkedin_topic = input("Enter your LinkedIn topic: ")
initial_state = {
"linkedin_topic": linkedin_topic,
"generated_post": [],
"human_feedback": []
}
stream = app.stream(initial_state, config=thread_config)
while True:
try:
chunk = next(stream)
# An interrupt arrives as a special event
if "__interrupt__" in chunk:
# Interactive loop until we resume
while True:
try:
user_feedback = input("Your feedback
[okay/done/custom]: ").strip()
except KeyboardInterrupt:
user_feedback = "done"
if not user_feedback:
user_feedback = "okay"
if user_feedback.lower() in DONE_SET:
app.invoke(Command(resume="done"), config=thread_config)
break
else:
app.invoke(Command(resume=user_feedback),
config=thread_config)
except StopIteration:
break
print("Finished.")
Enable checkpointing so the graph can pause and later resume with full state.
Compile the graph with the checkpointer attached.
Unique thread ID isolates this conversation. Use a stable ID to resume later runs.
Collect the topic interactively. You can also seed this from upstream logic.
Seed the state with empty lists so add_messages can append cleanly.
Start streaming execution to receive events, including interrupts.
266 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
Detect the interrupt marker and enter a local input loop until you resume.
Map user choice to the correct resume command. Done exits the loop. Any other
string continues the iteration.
Exit banner for a clean end state.
This multi-turn conversation example is just one of several patterns you can use. The
right pattern depends on the type of task, the level of risk, and the kind of oversight
you want to maintain. Following are some of the most common approaches, each
with a brief description followed by a code example you can adapt for your own
LangGraph workflow:
Approve or reject
Pause before a sensitive action, such as sending an API request or initiating a
financial transaction. Review the action and either approve it, reject it, or choose
an alternative path:
if low in {"approve", "yes", "ok", "okay"}:
return Command(update={"human_feedback": feedback_list + ["approved"]},
goto="model")
if low in {"reject", "no"}:
return Command(update={"human_feedback": feedback_list + ["rejected"]},
goto="end_node")
Review and edit
Instead of answering with a simple yes or no, you can make adjustments to the
data or instructions before allowing the agent to continue:
payload = {"draft": latest, "instructions": "Edit text or press Enter"}
user_feedback = interrupt(payload)
if isinstance(user_feedback, dict) and "draft" in user_feedback:
edited = user_feedback["draft"]
return Command(
update={
"generated_post": [AIMessage(content=edited)],
"human_feedback": feedback_list + ["edited"]
},
goto="model"
)
# fall back to normal feedback handling if needed
The Human Factor: Steering Agent Actions | 267
Review tool calls
If a tool call could affect external systems, inspect its arguments before execution.
For example, you might confirm booking details before a travel reservation is
made:
def tool_gate(state: State) -> Command:
proposed = {"tool": "book_flight", "args": state["proposed_args"]}
decision = interrupt({"review": proposed, "message":
"Approve or edit args"})
if isinstance(decision, dict) and decision.get("approve"):
return Command(goto="execute_tool")
if isinstance(decision, dict) and "args" in decision:
return Command(update={"proposed_args": decision["args"]},
goto="execute_tool")
return Command(update={"human_feedback": feedback_list +
["rejected tool call"]}, goto="end_node")
Validate human input
In some cases, the human is providing data to the system. HITL can validate that
data before the agent proceeds, ensuring accuracy and completeness:
def validate_input(state: State) -> Command:
errors = find_errors(state["form_data"]) # your validation logic
if errors:
fixes = interrupt({"errors": errors, "message": "Please correct fields"})
merged = {**state["form_data"], **(fixes or {})}
return Command(update={"form_data": merged}, goto="validate_input")
return Command(goto="model")
LangGraph provides extensive documentation on how you can apply these different
patterns in your own application.
As a rule of thumb, when implementing HITL, place any code with side effects such
as API calls or database writes after the approval step to avoid repeating irreversible
actions when a paused step is resumed.
Solving GitHub Issues with Coding Agents
Just as human developers benefit from integrated development environments (IDEs)
like PyCharm, coding agents can benefit from purpose-built interfaces that match
their strengths and mitigate their weaknesses. SWE-agent3 introduces an agent-
computer interface (ACI), a specialized interaction layer that abstracts away the
complexity of raw Linux shells and instead offers a streamlined set of language
3 John Yang et al. “SWE-agent: Agent-Computer Interfaces Enable Automated Software Engineering” (2024).
268 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
model–friendly commands for navigating, searching, and editing code. Figure 9-7
shows a simplified overview of SWE-agent.
Figure 9-7. Overview of SWE-agent and how it interacts with a computer via an agent-
computer interface (ACI) defining its commands and feedback format.
Rather than expecting the model to construct verbose, error-prone shell commands,
the ACI provides a compact and well-documented action space with commands like
find_file, search_file, goto, and edit, each designed for reliability and ease of
use. Guardrails such as syntax checking and automated error recovery prevent invalid
edits from propagating through the workflow. The environment feedback is concise
yet informative, giving the model only the context it needs to make its next decision
without overloading its token window.
With this interface in place, SWE-agent allows your chosen language model (such as
GPT-4o or Qwen3-Coder-480B-A35B) to autonomously use tools to fix issues in
your GitHub repositories, detecting cybersecurity vulnerabilities or carrying out any
other custom-defined task for you.
Mini SWE-agent
The developers of SWE-agent released a 100 times smaller version
of SWE-agent, called mini SWE-agent, which still works nearly as
well. This version is perfect for developers who like their tools like
their scripts: short, sharp, and readable. It’s also useful for engineers
who want something trivial to sandbox and to deploy anywhere.
You can easily install it via PyPI with pip install mini-swe-
agent. You can run it locally or with docker execution, and with
models like GPT-5, Claude Sonnet, or local models. The frame‐
work supports HITL and customized execution. More information
on how to run mini SWE-agent can be found in their cookbook.
Solving GitHub Issues with Coding Agents | 269
To run SWE-agent to solve your GitHub issues, you need to install it from the source
and then prepare the environment:
git clone https://github.com/SWE-agent/SWE-agent.git
SWE-agent $ python3.11 -m venv venv-sweagent
SWE-agent $ source venv-sweagent/bin/activate
(venv-sweagent) SWE-agent $ pip install --editable .
After that, you can simply run:
sweagent run \
--agent.model.name=gpt-5-mini \
--agent.model.per_instance_cost_limit=2.00 \
--env.repo.github_url=https://github.com/SWE-agent/test-repo \
--problem_statement.github_url=https://github.com/SWE-agent/test-repo/issues/1
Limit cost.
Repo link.
GitHub issue to be solved.
You aren’t limited to a single model when running SWE-agent. By changing the
--agent.model.name parameter, you can select from a range of LLMs, such as GPT
4o or Anthropic’s Claude Sonnet, depending on your performance, cost, or prefer‐
ence requirements. The same interface can also be paired with multimodal models,
enabling the agent to go beyond text-based reasoning and handle visual debugging
tasks, such as identifying issues in frontend layouts or fixing problems in data visuali‐
zations. What makes this possible is the ACI, which reduces the complexity of the
environment into a clear and consistent set of actions, allowing the agent to focus on
reasoning rather than low-level command construction.
This illustrates a broader principle of agent design. Autonomy is shaped as much by
the tools and feedback an agent receives as by the model’s internal capabilities.
Purpose-built interfaces such as ACI give agents the structure, guardrails, and concise
context they need to act reliably, adapt to feedback, and carry out complex
problem solving.
270 | Chapter 9: From Scripts to Thinking: AI Agents for Complex Tasks
Conclusion
In this chapter, you explored what autonomy means in today’s AI agents and how it’s
expressed through orchestrated decision making, tool use, and adaptive workflows.
While these systems are not yet fully self-directed, they can plan, act, and refine their
behavior in real time within the boundaries of a defined architecture.
You saw how autonomy is shaped by design, from routers that make single-step
choices to MASs where specialized agents collaborate under a supervisor. State
machines and frameworks like LangGraph provide the structure for these workflows,
supporting dynamic control flow, memory management, and coordination between
agents.
Context engineering emerged as a critical paradigm, ensuring that agents can act with
exactly the information they need, while human-in-the-loop patterns give you the
ability to guide decisions at key moments, balancing efficiency with safety and trust.
Memory proved to be as critical as planning or tool use. By combining procedural,
episodic, and semantic memory, agents can retain skills, recall past events, and
retrieve factual knowledge at the right moment. With mechanisms for local, shared,
and lifelong memory, agents can accumulate experience across sessions.
Finally, SWE-agent illustrated how purpose-built environments can amplify agent
performance by constraining the action space, standardizing feedback, and integrat‐
ing guardrails, making complex problem solving more reliable.
With these principles in place, you’re ready to move into Chapter 10, where the focus
turns to optimizing the transformer itself for problem solving, tuning its architecture,
reasoning strategies, and inference methods to push agent performance even further.
Conclusion | 271
CHAPTER 10
Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger:
Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
As you’ve seen throughout the book, transformers are generalists. They can generate
text, compose music, write code, and create or classify images. You’ve learned that
end-to-end reinforcement learning combined with chain of thought can spark inter‐
nal reasoning and help models solve more complex problems. You’ve also gained an
understanding that dividing problems into subproblems and allowing language mod‐
els to collaborate in a multi-agent system can turn them into robust tools for chal‐
lenging tasks.
Yet as task complexity grows, these capabilities are not enough. Extended reasoning,
multi-step decision making, and production-grade reliability expose weak points.
Without optimization, even the largest models or the most advanced agentic systems
can have problems with difficult reasoning chains, waste computation on trivial
problems, or fail to adapt in real-world deployments.
Optimization is the missing piece. If you think about it, world-class athletes don’t rely
on talent or regular practice alone. They depend on an ecosystem that includes targe‐
ted training, recovery, nutrition, and mental preparation. In the same way, transform‐
ers can’t rely only on pretraining or supervised fine-tuning. They need reinforcement
signals, adaptive reasoning strategies, efficient test-time scaling, and strong systems-
level infrastructure. In practice, this means going beyond the baseline with post-
training frameworks that refine policies and rewards, teaching models when to think
and when to act, scaling compute at inference to search effectively, and updating
weights quickly in production so policies improve while serving real workloads.
Table 10-1 summarizes this optimization ecosystem.
273
Table 10-1. Components of the optimization ecosystem for agentic LLMs
Concept Component(s) Role Example
Training-time ART, GRPO, RULER Refine reasoning, tool use, and decision Relative trajectory ranking
optimization policies with RL with RULER
Inference-time scaling AdaptThink, search Allocate compute by difficulty; decide Difficulty-gated CoT with
methods (e.g., ETS) when to think versus act AdaptThink
Open RL frameworks rLLM, verl Distributed rollouts, training, and Train coding agents with
evaluation GRPO
Systems-level Checkpoint Engine Distribute updated weights from learner Policy refresh in production
infrastructure to actors rapidly
The following sections walk through each part of this optimization ecosystem.
Training-time optimization shows how RL methods like ART, GRPO, and RULER
make agents more reliable. Inference-time scaling covers strategies such as Adapt‐
Think that cut wasted compute by deciding when extended reasoning is worthwhile.
Open RL frameworks like rLLM and verl illustrate how distributed infrastructure
brings these ideas into practice. And systems-level infrastructure with the Checkpoint
Engine shows how policies keep improving efficiently in production.
Training-Time Intelligence:
Reinforcement Learning for Agents
Training-time optimization is the first lever you have for making transformers more
reliable problem solvers. Pretraining and supervised fine-tuning establish broad com‐
petence, but RL provides the feedback loop that refines behavior against real objec‐
tives. Rather than relying on fixed labels or static datasets, RL evaluates the model’s
behavior dynamically: you score its outputs relative to desired goals and then update
weights accordingly.
Modern frameworks extend this principle beyond simple preference learning. Agent
reinforcement training (ART) structures rollouts around tool use and decision mak‐
ing. ART is an open source framework that trains agentic LLMs to enhance their per‐
formance and reliability through experience. It uses GRPO, which scales preference
learning to groups of candidate solutions and removes the need for absolute scores.
Relative universal LLM-elicited rewards (RULER) is a general-purpose reward func‐
tion that uses an LLM-as-judge to rank agent trajectories. It requires no labeled data,
expert supervision, or manually designed rewards, and it consistently improves agent
performance. Combine ART with RULER, and you can judge multiple trajectories
side by side, rewarding those that follow the best reasoning path or produce the most
useful final answer.
274 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
Together, these frameworks turn RL into a scalable engine for agent alignment, ena‐
bling models to refine reasoning strategies, tool usage patterns, and decision policies
at training time.
Beyond Hand-Crafted Rewards: How RULER Works
One of the biggest bottlenecks in RL for agents is reward design. Hand-crafted
reward functions are brittle, domain-specific, and expensive to maintain. LLM-as-
judge approaches have emerged as a flexible alternative, but they often suffer from
calibration problems: scores are inconsistent across runs, difficult to interpret, and
not always reliable enough to drive stable training. RULER addresses this gap by
reframing the problem in relative rather than absolute terms.
RULER works by ranking multiple candidate trajectories against one another, instead
of assigning absolute scores in isolation. It generates N trajectories for the same
prompt, each capturing the full sequence of inputs, outputs, and tool calls. RULER
deduplicates any shared prefixes (such as the system prompt) and passes the unique
suffixes to an LLM-as-judge, along with a rubric for evaluation. The judge scores each
trajectory from 0 to 1 based on whether it achieved its goal and how efficiently it did
so. These scores are then normalized within the group and used directly as rewards in
a GRPO update. The judge can also provide explanations for its scores, which you
can use to analyze faults and cluster low-performing runs. Figure 10-1 shows a high-
level overview of how RULER works.
Figure 10-1. RULER training loop: trajectories are ranked by an LLM-as-judge, normal‐
ized by GRPO, and used to update the policy iteratively.
The design uses two key insights:
• It’s easier for an LLM to rank multiple candidate solutions than to score them
individually. Side-by-side comparison highlights deficiencies that might not be
obvious in isolation.
• With GRPO, rewards only need to be consistent within a group of candidates.
Global calibration is unnecessary, which sidesteps one of the most persistent
challenges in LLM-as-judge setups.
In practice, its design makes RULER highly stable and scalable. It removes the need
for labeled data or expert-crafted rules while still producing signals strong enough to
Training-Time Intelligence: Reinforcement Learning for Agents | 275
guide complex policy updates. It also converges faster than hand-tuned baselines,
since it can give partial credit to incomplete but promising solutions.
Customizing RULER
Although RULER accepts custom rubrics for domain-specific
tasks, experience shows that the default rubric often works best. It
prioritizes goal completion, efficiency, and proportional scoring for
close versus large performance gaps. Rather than over-customizing
the rubric, it’s usually more effective to provide clear instructions in
the system prompt of the agent itself. Because RULER sees that
prompt as part of the trajectory, it can use it to align rankings with
the agent’s intended goals.
RULER’s relative scoring design also makes it suitable for inference-time scaling.
Instead of producing a single best answer, a model can generate multiple candidates
and let RULER select the most promising one on the fly. This bridges the gap between
training-time optimization and test-time reasoning; it allows online learning systems
to adapt continuously as agents are deployed.
Training in Practice: ART in a Market Scenario
To make this concrete, I’ll walk you through an example of how you can combine
ART and RULER in a trading environment. I’ll use ART here because it provides you
the conceptual framework for RL with agents, and it’s designed to be developer-
friendly. It offers you wrappers that make it straightforward to use RL training with
your existing applications without requiring you to rewrite large parts of your code.
The training server is abstracted as a modular service, so your application doesn’t
need to interface with it directly.
You can train from almost anywhere: run the ART client on your laptop and let the
server launch a GPU-enabled environment, or train on a local GPU if available. Inte‐
grations with hosted platforms such as Weights and Biases, Langfuse, and OpenPipe
provide flexible observability, logging, and debugging out of the box. ART also comes
with intelligent defaults for training parameters and inference settings, optimized for
efficiency and stability, while still letting you customize configurations for your spe‐
cific needs.
The example I’ll show is a simple agent that calculates a short-term moving average
(SMA) for stock prices using LangGraph for rollouts and RULER for reward scoring.
What matters is not the finance context but the pattern. Table 10-2 shows you some
ideas for adapting my example to support other functionality.

records

return_final_answer_tool document readers

pass/fail unit tests

prompts and toolsets
You can use the same loop for pretty much any agentic task where an LLM needs to
work with tools, create an output, and then be checked for success. The market SMA
agent is just an easy example to show you the patterns in action, so you can see how
they work and add them to your own projects.

answer with cited message IDs. As with the market SMA agent, it
uses LangGraph for rollouts and RULER for relative rewards, then
updates the policy with GRPO.
Now, let me walk you through how to combine ART and RULER in a financial trad‐

price data. The training process uses ART’s rollout abstraction and RULER’s trajec‐
tory ranking to optimize the agent’s behavior. I omit the tools and some other code
here, but you’ll find everything working together in the accompanying notebook.
First, you need to choose a model (Example 10-1) that ART can train. In this case, I’ll
use a Qwen 2.5 model, though you could just as well point to an Unsloth-optimized
4-bit variant if you want faster training on limited hardware, or any other model
you’d like.

Next, define a schema (Example 10-4) and a tool for the final answer, since agents
need a structured way to output results.
Example 10-4. Final answer setup
class FinalAnswer(BaseModel):
answer: str
source_ids: List[str]
nonlocal_final = {"value": None}
@tool
def return_final_answer_tool(answer: str, reference_ids: List[str]) -> dict:
nonlocal_final["value"] = FinalAnswer(answer=answer, source_ids=reference_ids)
return nonlocal_final["value"].model_dump()
To train the agent, you need a reward. Example 10-5 uses a numeric reward function.
This function compares the agent’s SMA prediction against the ground truth and
assigns a score.
Example 10-5. Numeric reward function
def judge_sma_numeric(ticker: str, window: int, period_days: int,
y_pred: str, tol: float=0.02) -> tuple[float, float, float]:
df = fetch_prices(ticker, period_days)
closes = df["Close"].tail(window).tolist()
if len(closes) < window:
return 0.0, float("nan"), float("nan")
truth = sum(closes)/window
try:
pred = float(re.findall(r"-?\d+(?:\.\d+)?", y_pred)[0])
except Exception:
return 0.0, truth, float("nan")
err = abs(pred - round(truth, 2))
reward = 1.0 if err <= tol else 0.0
return reward, truth, pred
Extracts the number string from the agent’s output, tolerating formatting noise.
Now you tie everything together in a rollout, as implemented in Example 10-6. The
rollout is the “unit of experience” for ART. Each rollout is a complete episode: the
agent receives a task, interacts with tools, produces an answer, and receives a reward.
This abstraction is domain-agnostic: whether the task is computing stock SMAs,
answering emails, or running tests, the rollout always follows the same structure.
Training-Time Intelligence: Reinforcement Learning for Agents | 279
Example 10-6. Rollout with LangGraph agent
@weave.op
async def rollout(model: art.Model,
market_scenario: MarketScenario) -> ProjectTrajectory:
scn = market_scenario.scenario
nonlocal_final["value"] = None
traj = ProjectTrajectory(
reward=0.0,
messages_and_choices=[],
metadata={"scenario_id": scn.id, "ticker": scn.ticker,
"window": scn.window},
)
system_prompt = dedent(f"""
You are a market data agent.
Use tools to fetch prices and compute the {scn.window}-day SMA:
- Call latest_closes_tool(ticker="{scn.ticker}",
n={scn.window}, period_days={scn.period_days})
- Compute SMA = average of those closes.
- Use calculator for exact arithmetic, e.g. '(x+y+z)/{scn.window}'
- Round to 2 decimals.
- MUST call return_final_answer_tool with ONLY the number
string (e.g., "240.79").
Constraints:
- Do not guess. Always call latest_closes_tool first.
- Do NOT add text; final answer tool gets only the number string.
""")
tools = [latest_closes_tool, calculator, return_final_answer_tool]
chat = init_chat_model(model.name, temperature=0.2, max_tokens=200)
agent = create_react_agent(chat, tools)
try:
config = {
"configurable": {"thread_id": str(uuid.uuid4())},
"recursion_limit": MAX_TURNS,
}
await agent.ainvoke(
{"messages": [SystemMessage(content=system_prompt),
HumanMessage(content=scn.question)]},
config=config,
)
if nonlocal_final["value"]:
traj.final_answer = nonlocal_final["value"]
reward, truth, pred = judge_sma_numeric(
scn.ticker, scn.window, scn.period_days,
280 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
traj.final_answer.answer, tol=scn.tolerance
)
traj.reward = reward
traj.metrics["truth"] = round(truth, 6) if truth == truth else None
traj.metrics["pred"] = pred
traj.metrics["error"] = (abs(round(truth,2)-pred) if
truth==truth and pred==pred else None)
traj.metrics["correct"] = float(reward)
except Exception as e:
print("Rollout error:", e)
traj.messages_and_choices.append({"role": "assistant",
"content": f"Error: {e}"})
return traj
Resets the final answer holder at the start of each rollout.
Attaches metadata so you can later slice results by scenario or ticker.
Encodes workflow constraints in the prompt to enforce tool discipline.
Binds the model to only the tools you expose.
Gives each rollout its own thread ID and recursion limit for clean logs and safety.
Attaches rewards and metrics directly to the trajectory so training and evaluation
remain coupled.
The same rollout design works beyond finance:
• Replace latest_closes_tool with a search API or a document reader.
• Replace the numeric SMA reward with, for example, a pass/fail unit test.
The agent still follows the same structure: prompt → tools → answer → reward.
Once you have rollouts, you can generate batches, assign rewards, and train the
model. You can see how to implement this in Example 10-7. The training objective
then turns these rollouts into gradient updates via GRPO, which essentially starts by
sampling prompts and generating completions. The reward model then evaluates
these completions to compute individual rewards r. These rewards are group-
i
normalized across completions for each prompt, where r values are centered and
i
scaled to produce relative comparisons that serve as the policy update signal.
Next, the Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence term, introduced in “Diffusion Models:
What’s That Noise About?” on page 99, is estimated between the current and refer‐
ence policies. It approximates how much the probability distribution Q of the upda‐
ted policy differs from the true or reference distribution P. In practice, this KL term
Training-Time Intelligence: Reinforcement Learning for Agents | 281
acts as a regularizer that penalizes the model for drifting too far from its reference
distribution while still allowing meaningful improvement. Finally, the model optimi‐
zes the mean GRPO loss, defined as the reward minus the KL penalty, which com‐
bines both terms into a single objective. Figure 10-2 illustrates a high-level overview
of this flow.
Figure 10-2. High-level GRPO mechanics: group-normalized rewards plus reference-
policy KL as a mean loss.
Example 10-7. Training loop with numeric rewards
training_config = {
"groups_per_step": 1,
"num_epochs": 2,
"rollouts_per_group": 4,
"learning_rate": 1e-5,
"max_steps": 3,
}
training_iterator = iterate_dataset(
scenarios,
groups_per_step=training_config["groups_per_step"],
num_epochs=training_config["num_epochs"],
initial_step=await model.get_step(),
)
for batch in training_iterator:
print(f"Training step {batch.step}, epoch {batch.epoch}")
groups = []
for scn in batch.items:
group = art.TrajectoryGroup(
[wrap_rollout(model, rollout)(model,
MarketScenario(step=batch.step, scenario=scn))
for _ in range(training_config["rollouts_per_group"]) ]
)
groups.append(group)
finished = await art.gather_trajectory_groups(
groups, pbar_desc="gather",
max_exceptions=training_config["rollouts_per_group"] * len(batch.items),
282 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
)
print_group_summary(finished, "Pre judge group summary")
judged = finished
print_group_summary(judged, "Post judge group summary")
await model.train(
judged,
config=art.TrainConfig(learning_rate=training_config["learning_rate"]),
_config={"logprob_calculation_chunk_size": 8},
)
print(f"Completed training step {batch.step}")
if batch.step >= training_config["max_steps"]:
break
Training is mini-batched into groups per step. Each group bundles several roll‐
outs for stability.
iterate_dataset handles sampling and ensures reproducibility across epochs.
Each scenario spawns multiple rollouts → turned into a TrajectoryGroup. This
makes GRPO-style relative scoring possible.
gather executes rollouts concurrently and collects results safely, handling partial
failures.
You’ve already computed numeric rewards in rollout.
Each batch ends with a gradient update, informed by the reward signal.
GRPO Intuition
GRPO works by collecting multiple candidate answers for the same task and ranking
them. The learning signal comes from these relative rankings, not from absolute
scores. This makes training more stable, because the model only needs to improve
within each group of candidates.
The update also includes a regularization term that keeps the new policy close to a
reference, so learning is steady and safe.
The result is a reinforcement learning method that scales well, avoids brittle hand-
crafted rewards, and steadily improves reasoning and tool use.
Training-Time Intelligence: Reinforcement Learning for Agents | 283
Finally, you can swap out the numeric reward for RULER’s relative scoring, as shown
in Example 10-8. This turns your setup into a scalable, general-purpose RL loop.
Example 10-8. RULER scoring integration
judged_groups = []
for group in finished:
judged_group = await ruler_score_group(group, "openai/o4-mini", debug=True)
judged_groups.append(judged_group)
await model.train(
judged_groups,
config=art.TrainConfig(learning_rate=training_config["learning_rate"]),
_config={"logprob_calculation_chunk_size": 8},
)
RULER deduplicates shared prefixes and applies relative scoring using an LLM-
as-judge.
The ranked trajectory groups are then used directly for policy updates.
Chunk size is lowered for stable log-prob calculations on long sequences.
Prevent Leakage
Guard against leakage by prohibiting tools from fetching the gold
answer during judging to keep the judge prompt deterministic.
Leakage occurs when the evaluation prompt or an external tool
indirectly exposes the ground-truth solution to the model. This can
lead the LLM-as-judge to overfit or memorize answers instead of
genuinely assessing reasoning quality, producing inflated scores
and unstable training signals. The gold answer is the ground-truth
solution used for evaluation. To avoid leakage, ensure that the
judge prompt is fully deterministic and that all inputs available to
the LLM are limited to the agent’s raw trajectories and the evalua‐
tion rubric.
As you’ve seen, the ART-LangGraph integration makes it simple to build AI agents
that get smarter with experience, helping you turn rough prototypes into reliable,
real-world systems. And tracking the experiments with Weights & Biases or Lang‐
Fuse, this gives you a good way to improve your agents and the underlying LLMs.
However, strong policies still should not overthink easy inputs. Overthinking is when
an LLM performs unnecessary reasoning steps on easy inputs, which increases com‐
putational cost without improving accuracy. The good news is LLMs can be trained
284 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
for selective reasoning, enabling them to allocate reasoning only when the task
requires it.
Reason Smarter, Not Harder:
Adaptive Compute Allocation
While systems like ART and RULER teach models how to reason effectively, they still
apply complex, multi-step processes to every task. This leads to an efficiency bottle‐
neck: forcing an LLM to perform an extended CoT reasoning path for a simple prob‐
lem wastes compute, increases latency, and can even introduce “overthinking” that
could degrade the final answer.
An easy way to address this and help your models decide when to think is
AdaptThink.1 AdaptThink teaches the model to allocate compute strategically and
adapt for difficulty. It moves the model from being a purely reactive reasoner to a
strategic resource manager that adapts its inference strategy according to task com‐
plexity. It has two modes:
Thinking mode
A verbose, CoT-style process for complex problems, where the computational
cost is justified by the higher accuracy
NoThinking mode
Provides a direct, concise final answer and is often more efficient and performant
for straightforward problems
The core insight from the paper is that the benefits of CoT are not always given;
instead, they become helpful only when the problem is actually hard.
The Delta Incentive: Enforcing Efficiency
To teach the model this strategic allocation, AdaptThink uses a specialized RL objec‐
tive that encodes a cost-benefit analysis directly into the reward signal. This mecha‐
nism relies on two components:
• The policy is penalized if its overall accuracy drops below that of a reference
model.
• The model receives a structural bonus, delta, for choosing the highly efficient
NoThinking mode.
1 Jiajie Zhang et al. “AdaptThink: Reasoning Models Can Learn When to Think” (2025).
Reason Smarter, Not Harder: Adaptive Compute Allocation | 285
This creates a competitive dynamic where the model is mathematically pushed
toward the cheapest answer unless the expected performance gain from thinking out‐
weighs the efficiency bonus. The policy must at least match the accuracy of a frozen
reference model while earning a structural δ-bonus whenever it selects NoThinking.
In practice, δ is a dial: higher δ favors efficiency (more NoThinking), while lower δ
favors accuracy (more Thinking), allowing you to adjust the policy to match your
latency or token-cost requirements. Figure 10-3 illustrates the concept.
Figure 10-3. AdaptThink difficulty gate: route easy inputs to NoThinking (speed) and
hard ones to Thinking (accuracy).
The official repository provides end-to-end training scripts, but it’s helpful to under‐
stand what each stage actually does. The first step is to check the tokenizer templates.
Both <think> and </think> must appear at the correct positions in tokenizer_con
fig.json; otherwise, the gating mechanism that distinguishes Thinking and NoThink
ing modes will silently fail. Once you’ve verified the template, the next step is to
presample reference results. Here, you run the frozen reference model multiple times
on each training problem to compute an instance-level accuracy baseline. This base‐
line is important: it anchors the optimization by ensuring that the trained model can’t
go below the reference accuracy already achieved.
Training itself is launched using either single-node or multi-node scripts, depending
on model size, with a chosen value of δ determining how aggressively the policy is
biased toward NoThinking responses.
286 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
How to Tune
δ
As a rule of thumb, set δ according to how much efficiency you’re willing to trade for
accuracy:
• Around 0.02, the model remains conservative, favoring thinking and closely pre‐
serving accuracy.
• Near 0.05, you usually get the best balance, with shorter responses and stable
accuracy.
• Beyond 0.075, the policy becomes aggressive, maximizing NoThinking and effi‐
ciency but often at the expense of performance on harder tasks.
After training, checkpoints are converted to Hugging Face format for evaluation, and
the supplied scripts allow you to measure the accuracy, response length, and NoThink
ing ratio consistently.
By using AdaptThink, you shift your model from applying uniform reasoning to
applying reasoning selectively, based on task difficulty. The reinforcement signal you
set ensures that efficiency gains never come at the expense of overall accuracy, while
the δ-incentive lets you control the cost/performance balance. The outcome is a
model that can maintain high accuracy on complex problems while avoiding unnec‐
essary computation on simpler ones.
Open Innovation: Community-Driven RL Frameworks
RL for agents is not just advancing inside corporate labs; it’s actively being shaped by
open source frameworks like RL for language agents (rLLM). rLLM is a high-
throughput RL infrastructure giving researchers and engineers access to the same
architectural setups as proprietary models so they can run distributed sampling, inte‐
grate custom tools, and scale post-training for language agents.
rLLM’s interface addresses the full lifecycle of agent development, the training phase
and the real-world deployment for production workloads. The Python-based frame‐
work focuses on distributed RL infrastructure to deliver efficient trajectory genera‐
tion and scalable RL training through specialized execution mechanisms. Table 10-3
maps the classic sampler-trainer loop into rLLM’s modular components.

rLLM provides recipes that train coding agents like DeepCoder, which reached 60.6%

rLLM provides utilities for your entire workflow. With DatasetRegistry, you can
register datasets once, standardize preprocessing, and store them in Parquet format
for both inference and training. For example, math or coding benchmarks can be
normalized into LiveCodeBench-style fields with a small preprocessing function, as
shown in Example 10-9, and then plugged directly into agents for training or
evaluation.

def _preprocess_fn(example, idx):
tests, metadata, starter_code = _normalize_tests(example)
problem = example.get("problem") or example.get("prompt") or
example.get("question") or ""
question = fetch_live_code_bench_system_prompt(problem, starter_code if
starter_code else None)
return {
"question": question,
"ground_truth": json.dumps(tests),
"data_source": "livecodebench",
"uid": f"deepcoder_{idx}",
"index": idx,
"starter_code": starter_code,
"metadata": json.dumps(metadata),
}

With preprocessing in place, you can register your train splits and your test splits via
DatasetRegistry so rLLM can load them by key. The function in Example 10-10 and
the CLI in Example 10-11 take care of preparing the data for rLLM.
Example 10-10. Register and shard the dataset
def prepare_deepcoder_data(train_size: int = 0, test_size: int = 0,
num_proc: int = 8):
name = "agentica-org/DeepCoder-Preview-Dataset"
train_parts = [ds for cfg in TRAIN_CONFIGS if
(ds := _safe_load(name, cfg, "train")) is not None]
if not train_parts:
raise RuntimeError("No train splits found among primeintellect, taco, lcbv5")
test_parts = [ds for cfg in TEST_CONFIGS if
(ds := _safe_load(name, cfg, "test")) is not None]
if not test_parts:
# fallback: carve a small test slice out of train concat
test_parts = [train_parts[0].select(range(min(100, len(train_parts[0]))))]
train_ds = concatenate_datasets(train_parts)
test_ds = concatenate_datasets(test_parts)
if train_size and train_size > 0:
train_ds = train_ds.select(range(min(train_size, len(train_ds))))
if test_size and test_size > 0:
test_ds = test_ds.select(range(min(test_size, len(test_ds))))
train_ds = train_ds.map(_preprocess_fn, with_indices=True,
writer_batch_size=16, num_proc=num_proc)
test_ds = test_ds.map(_preprocess_fn, with_indices=True,
writer_batch_size=16, num_proc=num_proc)
train_reg = DatasetRegistry.register_dataset("deepcoder", train_ds, "train")
test_reg = DatasetRegistry.register_dataset("deepcoder_test", test_ds, "test")
return train_reg, test_reg

if not args.limit or args.limit <= 0:
tasks = test_dataset.repeat(n=args.repeat)

base = getattr(test_dataset, "get_data", lambda: None)()
if base is None:
one_pass = test_dataset.repeat(n=1)
base_slice = one_pass[: args.limit]
else:
k = min(args.limit, len(base))
base_slice = [base[i] for i in range(k)]
tasks = base_slice * max(1, args.repeat)
