In this example, the different pieces of rLLM came together to form a full training
and evaluation loop. The ToolAgent provided the reasoning layer with access to
Python execution, while the ToolEnvironment ensured that this execution happened
296 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
in a safe and controlled sandbox. Datasets were prepared and managed centrally
through the DatasetRegistry, making them easy to register once and reuse for both
evaluation and training. For evaluation, the AgentExecutionEngine enabled efficient,
parallel rollouts that produced pass@k metrics across many tasks. Finally, the Agent
Trainer orchestrated the RL process itself, applying GRPO on top of the agent and
environment to steadily improve its tool use. Together, these components give you an
easy way to refine the agent’s performance through RL.
The Checkpoint Engine: Systems-Level
Optimization for LLM Policy Updates
The success of a scalable RL system relies on a high-throughput, low-latency loop for
policy updates. The open source middleware Checkpoint Engine3 is designed to solve
this bottleneck of transferring updated model weights between the training step and
the generation step, enabling faster iteration for RL. The engine facilitates a hybrid
colocated architecture where the entire RL process is contained within the same
worker nodes: a training engine (the learner) and an inference engine (the actor)
reside side by side. This physical proximity minimizes data transfer overhead, which
is important to optimize training of larger models. Figure 10-5 shows an overview of
this training process via the checkpoint engine.
Figure 10-5. Model parameter update using the checkpoint engine. Image taken from
Yifan Bai et al. (2025).
In modern RL for agentic systems, the inference engine generates new agent trajecto‐
ries using the current policy (acting as the actor). The training engine then uses this
3 Yifan Bai et al. “Kimi K2: Open Agentic Intelligence” (2025).
The Checkpoint Engine: Systems-Level Optimization for LLM Policy Updates | 297
data to compute a new, improved policy (acting as the learner). The system must effi‐
ciently shift between these two engines, updating the model’s weights in the process.
The checkpoint engine facilitates this fast transition, enabling an efficient, high-
frequency RL loop.
The core of the checkpoint engine is the ParameterServer service, which is designed
to run colocated with the LLM inference engine (such as vLLM) on the same GPU
workers. This service offers two primary, optimized methods for distributing model
weights. The broadcast method is the fastest and default, intended for synchronous
updates across a cluster. It uses a high-throughput, pipelined data transfer strategy,
including host-to-device (H2D) transfer, inter-worker broadcast, and reloading from
a shared CUDA IPC buffer, to apply the new policy. However, due to hardware con‐
straints like PCIe saturation on devices such as NVIDIA H800 clusters, concurrent
H2D and broadcast operations are often not possible. The engine therefore adopts a
practical two-stage scheme: all devices perform a single, synchronous H2D transfer,
followed by the broadcast and reload proceeding in parallel, as illustrated in
Figure 10-6.
Figure 10-6. Overlapping H2D transfer and weight update in the checkpoint engine.
Image taken from Yifan Bai et al. (2025).
The alternative method, peer-to-peer (P2P) is designed for greater robustness, allow‐
ing new instances to asynchronously join the cluster without disrupting the ongoing
rollouts of existing workers. By abstracting this, the checkpoint engine supports effec‐
tive RL for LLMs and agentic systems.
In practice, this means that models are not only trained more efficiently but can also
be kept fresh in deployed environments. With rapid distribution of updated weights,
actors run on the latest policy almost immediately, closing the loop between learning
and deployment. This ability to iterate continuously without interrupting service is
what turns RL from a research workflow into a production-grade system.
298 | Chapter 10: Smarter, Better, Faster, Stronger: Optimizing LLMs and AI Agents
Conclusion
In this chapter, you saw how RL converts objectives into behavior, with ART provid‐
ing a developer-friendly rollout loop, GRPO enabling stable group-based updates,
and RULER turning LLM-as-judge into a reliable relative reward. Together, these
methods align reasoning, tool use, and decision policies so agents improve through
experience.
You learned that speed and accuracy are not enemies if the model learns when to
think. AdaptThink introduces selective reasoning with a simple idea: prefer the
cheapest valid path, switching into deliberate chains of thought only when the
expected gain justifies it. By tuning the δ, you can match policy behavior to your
latency or cost budget while preserving accuracy on hard problems.
Open frameworks make RL practical. With rLLM, you can register datasets, run high-
throughput rollouts, score trajectories, and train agents with PPO or GRPO. Bench‐
marks for coding, math, and software engineering provide grounded evaluation so
progress is measurable and repeatable.
Finally, you looked at systems-level plumbing. Checkpoint engine closes the loop
between acting and learning by moving updated weights to inference servers quickly
and safely. This keeps the policy fresh in production settings and enables continuous
improvement without service disruption.
With these principles and tools, you’re ready to move from optimization to deploy‐
ment. Chapter 11 focuses on putting transformer models into production. You’ll
learn how to serve models efficiently, monitor quality and drift, manage safety and
guardrails, and set up rollbacks and cost controls for real workloads.
Conclusion | 299
CHAPTER 11
Deploying Transformer Models
Now that you know about different transformer models, it’s time to put them to
work. Deploying transformers with tools like vLLM or SGLang has become straight‐
forward on most major cloud platforms, such as GCP or AWS. Therefore, this chap‐
ter doesn’t focus on cloud deployment instructions but on the objectives that kept me
up at night when I first deployed transformer-based application, such as cost and data
control, runtime engineering, cache behavior, architecture-specific constraints, and
infrastructure strategy.
With this in mind, one of the first choices that shapes how you set up your applica‐
tion is whether you rely on open or closed models. This choice is not a purely techni‐
cal question; it determines your organization’s ability to control costs, scale flexibly,
audit behavior, customize models, and maintain long-term independence.
It’s also important to understand that deploying a transformer isn’t simply a matter of
taking an open source checkpoint and calling .generate. In production, a model
becomes just one component in a larger system that includes routing, storage, moni‐
toring, guardrails, and infrastructure. And even if you choose open source models,
this does not automatically mean self-hosting. Most open models can also be accessed
through managed cloud APIs, allowing fast experimentation before eventually mov‐
ing to self-hosted deployments for cost efficiency or compliance.
Once you decide how you want to access your model—closed API, managed open
source API, or fully self-hosted—the next step is understanding the architecture
you’re deploying. Different transformer families have different runtime, memory, and
concurrency characteristics, which directly affect cost, latency, and operational
behavior. Finally, you may want to optimize them through quantization.
301
This chapter provides a structured way to think about these decisions. The following
list acts as a simple roadmap that applies across modalities and helps you move trans‐
formers from ideation to production in an organized way:
1. Define the task, constraints, and modality.
2. Choose an appropriate architecture and model family.
3. Decide on open versus closed deployments.
4. Optimize the model for inference (quantization, pruning, sharding).
5. Engineer the inference runtime, including cache and thinking modes.
6. Integrate monitoring, benchmarking, and guardrails.
7. Choose and operate the infrastructure layer.
In the remainder of this chapter, I’ll guide you through the open-versus-closed
choice, explain what to watch for in different transformer architectures, and show
you how to optimize models for real-world production environments. The goal is to
give you guidance that helps you navigate deployment.
Choosing Between Open and Closed Source
Closed models offer strong baseline performance and immediate usability but at the
cost of opacity and vendor dependence. Open models provide visibility and control
yet traditionally required infrastructure experience to deploy. However, the distinc‐
tion is no longer binary. Most open source models can be accessed through managed
cloud APIs, such as Together.ai, Novita.ai, or Nebius, allowing teams to experiment
quickly without running their own GPU clusters.
In practice, the deployment spectrum ranges from fully closed models accessed via
proprietary APIs, to open models accessed via managed cloud providers, to self-
hosted open models that give you full control over cost, compliance, and optimiza‐
tion. Both open and closed models coexist in many organizations: closed models for
rapid prototyping or broad general tasks and open models for scalable, auditable,
domain-specific systems that need to evolve over time. In practice, many teams start
with open models via managed cloud providers and later migrate workloads that
require direct data access to self-hosted environments. Table 11-1 gives you an orien‐
tation on comparing open source and closed source models.
302 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Table 11-1. Open versus closed models: a practical comparison
Factor Closed source models Open source models
Speed of Depends on vendor release cycles; immediate Global research drives rapid iteration; usually available
innovation API access via both cloud APIs and self-hosting
Accessibility Simple API usage; limited visibility into data, Full visibility and auditability; accessible via Hugging
weights, or architecture Face, cloud APIs, or local deployment
Customization Fine-tuning possible inside vendor Full control over weights, training workflow, and
environment; no control over weights or inference runtime
runtime
Performance Often achieve top benchmarks due to Performance gap narrowing quickly; modern models
proprietary compute/training advantages (Qwen-3, Kimi K2) reach near-parity
Cost High per-token pricing; predictable but Low per-token cost via managed OSS providers; lowest
potentially expensive at scale long-term cost when self-hosted
Data security and Relies on vendor certifications; limited control Full control when self-hosted; configurable compliance
compliance over data residency for regulated industries
Operational burden Very low; vendor handles infrastructure Varies: zero when using managed APIs; higher for self-
hosting
This often leads to a hybrid approach. Early in a project’s lifecycle, managed closed
APIs or managed open source providers allow you to iterate quickly without commit‐
ting to infrastructure. Later, as your team expands and your use cases demand more
control or customization, it becomes clear that it’s better to bring open source deploy‐
ments in-house. At that stage, owning your infrastructure, fine-tuning models, and
optimizing inference becomes a core competence rather than an operational burden,
and can, in fact, lower costs over time.
Understanding the Architecture You’re Deploying
Another important part in moving from proof of concept to actual deployment is to
understand that different transformer architectures exhibit different runtime, mem‐
ory, and cost behaviors. This is why you need to be aware about these constraints and
deploy accordingly. In general, your deployment strategy must align with the under‐
lying architecture:
Decoder-only models (LLMs, code models, some time series transformers)
These models generate outputs autoregressively and rely heavily on key-value
(KV) caching to avoid recomputing attention over previously processed tokens.
Their runtime behavior is dominated by context length, batching strategy, and
scheduling efficiency. They also support different thinking modes, such as chain-
of-thought, multi-sample decoding, or tool calls, all of which increase inference
cost by triggering additional forward passes. These model architectures don’t just
Understanding the Architecture You’re Deploying | 303
exist for LLMs but also for time series. TimesFM1 is, for instance, a decoder-only
time series foundation model.
Encoder-only models (vision transformers, retrieval encoders, some anomaly detectors)
These models operate in a feed-forward, stateless manner, making them naturally
batch-friendly and high throughput. Since there’s no temporal state to maintain
across requests, latency is predictable, memory usage is easier to control, and
scaling is straightforward. They’re typically used for embedding extraction, clas‐
sification, and retrieval.
Encoder-decoder models (machine translation, diffusion pipelines, video transformer
stacks, end-to-end multimodal)
These systems perform multi-stage inference, with an encoder producing a latent
representation that conditions the decoder. They often rely on latent caching,
cross-attention, or repeated denoising steps, depending on the modality. Their
memory footprint is higher than encoder-only models, but many parts of their
computation can be parallelized across devices or stages.
Specialized architectures (latent diffusion, DiT, ViT backbones, PatchTST, video
diffusion transformers)
These models introduce modality-specific runtime characteristics. Latent diffu‐
sion models reuse text embeddings and latent states across sampling steps, time
series transformers reuse rolling hidden states, and video models cache temporal
features across frames. Their performance and memory behavior are shaped less
by token length and more by spatial and temporal resolution, making memory
planning and batching critical for stable deployment.
Understanding these architectural differences is essential because each family impo‐
ses its own constraints on latency, memory usage, batching efficiency, and cost. Once
you know which architecture you’re working with, you can design a deployment
strategy that fits its behavior rather than treating all transformers as interchangeable.
With that in mind, in the following sections I’ll cover more deeply some specialties
you need to think about when you’re deploying different transformer architectures.
1 Abhimanyu Das et al. “A Decoder-Only Foundation Model for Time-Series Forecasting” (2024).
304 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Deploying Decoder-Only Models
Decoder-only models, such as LLMs and code models, operate autoregressively and
therefore have a very distinct runtime profile. Figure 11-1 shows an overview of this
autoregressive process.
Figure 11-1. Abstracted overview of decoder-only architecture.
Since these models rely heavily on KV caching to avoid recomputing attention over
the entire sequence, their overall performance and cost are dominated by context
length, scheduling decisions, and how efficiently the cache is managed. From a
deployment perspective, the key property of decoder-only models is that they reuse
previously computed attention projections. Without additional optimizations, each
step would require recomputing all earlier attention states.
KV caching improves latency and throughput but also introduces a growing memory
footprint. Cache size scales with sequence length × layers × heads, which means
memory pressure becomes one of the primary bottlenecks when deploying large
decoder-only models.
Deploying Decoder-Only Models | 305
Modern models mitigate this through architectural choices such as grouped-query
attention (GQA), where many query heads share a smaller number of KV heads. You
can inspect this configuration easily via model.config in Hugging Face. For example,
Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-1M uses:
"num_attention_heads": 28,
"num_key_value_heads": 4,
This configuration reduces KV cache size significantly, as you do serve 7 query heads
for each KV pair, which is ~ 7× less KV memory.
In practice, KV cache management is one of the main indicators of whether a system
scales gracefully or collapses under load. For example, poorly controlled cache growth
will increase memory fragmentation, reduce scheduling efficiency, and decrease
concurrency.
Production Gotcha of Increasing Batch Size
The hidden bottleneck here isn’t compute; it’s memory bandwidth.
Increasing batch size looks free on paper until you hit the KV cache
limit, at which point performance falls off a cliff.
You can also reduce memory usage through prefix reuse. During beam search or
multi-sample decoding, models often share long initial prefixes. Reusing their KV
caches rather than recomputing them results in substantial performance gains. Like‐
wise, once the context grows beyond its useful range, techniques such as prefix trim‐
ming or sliding-window attention prevent KV caches from ballooning to the model’s
maximum context size.
Runtime Engineering for Decoder-Only Models
In deployment, you should monitor cache behavior continuously. The cache hit ratio
reveals whether the system is recomputing tokens unnecessarily or invalidating
caches too aggressively. Memory pressure exposes when KV usage approaches device
limits and signals that paging or trimming strategies may be required. Fragmentation
is another recurring concern, since unmanaged KV growth can reduce GPU memory
locality and degrade throughput. Frameworks such as vLLM provide optimized
mechanisms to manage KV caches efficiently. Table 11-2 summarizes the most
important ones.
306 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Table 11-2. vLLM cache management features
Mechanism Description Benefit
Paged attention Splits the KV cache into fixed-size memory blocks Prevents fragmentation; supports
managed by a free_block_queue long-running sessions
Prefix caching Hash-based reuse of shared prefixes Eliminates redundant prefill
computation
Chunked prefill Breaks long prefills into incremental chunks Improves concurrency; avoids cache
monopolization
Continuous batching Joint scheduling of prefill and decode Maintains consistent throughput
across mixed workloads
Hash-based Ensures that KV blocks remain correct after context Prevents reuse of stale or mismatched
verification changes KV blocks
Dynamic cache Returns KV blocks as soon as a request ends Keeps GPU utilization predictable and
reclamation stable
Disaggregated KV Shares KV across workers via shared storage Enables distributed inference setups
transfer
Another thing you’d want to look at is that any structural change in the input context
can invalidate portions of the KV cache. Token insertions, document injection, reor‐
dered segments, or trimmed prefixes all misalign the cached keys and values with the
model’s positional encodings. This leads to incoherent outputs unless the cache is
selectively invalidated.
Modern systems therefore combine the following:
• Sliding-window caching for bounded attention
• Segment-level caching to separate system prompts, retrieved chunks, and user
turns
• Context hashing to detect mismatches
This preserves correctness while retaining the performance benefits of caching.
The key insight I would like you to take away from this section is that efficient
deployment of decoder-only models isn’t all about retraining or quantizing them or
adding more GPUs; it’s mostly about engineering the inference runtime.
Security Considerations for Decoder-Only Deployments
When deploying decoder-only models at scale, especially with distributed inference,
security becomes an important concern. vLLM and similar inference servers rely
heavily on inter-node communication, and these communication paths are insecure
by default. This affects PyTorch Distributed traffic, KV cache transfers, tensor paral‐
lelism, and pipeline parallelism.
Deploying Decoder-Only Models | 307
None of these channels include built-in encryption or authentication. Messages are
sent in clear text, no authorization checks are performed, and services often bind to
all network interfaces unless explicitly configured otherwise. For this reason, dis‐
tributed inference must never be exposed to untrusted networks.
To deploy decoder-only models safely, follow these guidelines:
Network isolation
Run all vLLM nodes on a private, firewalled network. Only the public API port
should ever be exposed externally. Internal ports used for distributed training,
data-parallel coordination, or KV cache transfer must remain unreachable from
untrusted sources.
Explicit configuration
Set communication IPs explicitly using VLLM_HOST_IP, --kv-ip, data_paral
lel_master_ip, and related options instead of relying on defaults. Defaults often
bind to 0.0.0.0 and expose internal services unintentionally.
Firewall rules
Allow only necessary internal ports between trusted nodes. Block all other
inbound connections. Never expose PyTorch Distributed ports or KV cache
transfer ports to the public internet.
Restrict media domain access
Use --allowed-media-domains to restrict external URLs the model can fetch
during tool calls or image retrieval. Disable redirects with
VLLM_MEDIA_URL_ALLOW_REDIRECTS=0 to avoid server-side request forgery
(SSRF).
Least privilege access
Limit shell, Kubernetes, or machine-level access to only those who require it.
Distributed inference frameworks assume trusted environments; your infrastruc‐
ture must enforce that trust boundary.
In short, distributed inference workloads are powerful but inherently unsafe when
exposed directly, regardless of whether they serve decoder-only, encoder-only, or
multimodal architectures. Proper network segmentation and strict firewall configura‐
tion are essential components of any secure inference deployment.
Building Applications with Coding Models
Coding models introduce a fundamentally different deployment challenge than lan‐
guage, vision, or audio models. While they often share the same decoder-only archi‐
tecture with LLMs, their risk profile changes the moment the model is allowed to
inspect, modify, or execute code. At that point, the primary concern is no longer
308 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
decoding speed but the safety and containment of the execution environment in
which the model operates.
From a deployment perspective, coding models behave less like text generators and
more like agents with system-level capabilities. They write files, generate scripts,
invoke compilers, run tests, interact with package managers, or access external tools.
Deploying such a model therefore becomes a question of granting the model the min‐
imum necessary capabilities while constraining its operating environment in a way
that prevents damage, privilege escalation, or system compromise.
Safe deployment begins with strict sandboxing. Any code produced by a model must
run inside an isolated environment with restricted filesystem access, network con‐
trols, resource limits, and non-root user permissions. The Dockerfile in Example 11-1
creates a controlled sandbox for executing model-generated code with an MCP server
to run Python code in a sandbox (see GitHub). Every instruction supports one of
three goals: minimize the attack surface, ensure deterministic behavior, and enforce
least privilege.
Production Gotcha: The “Internal Network” Fallacy
A common mistake is assuming that because a model is running
inside a VPC or a private cluster, it’s safe. It isn’t. If a coding model
can execute code, it can scan your internal network, access meta‐
data services (like AWS Instant Metadata Service [IMDS]), or hit
internal admin dashboards. Zero trust is the only safe default for
agentic execution environments.
In the Dockerfile, you start from a slim Python base image to reduce installed soft‐
ware and to keep the runtime predictable. Environment variables disable pip caching
and define install paths so the image remains self-contained. Only the required OS
packages are installed, and all apt metadata is removed immediately to keep the foot‐
print small.
Deno, an open source JavaScript runtime, is installed as a fixed version and validated
at build time using a small inline script. This guarantees that the interpreter works
before the container reaches production. The MCP server is then installed along with
additional Python dependencies using binary wheels whenever possible to avoid frag‐
ile compilation paths.
The image switches to a dedicated non-root user, which is essential because model-
generated code will execute inside the container. This prevents access to system direc‐
tories and limits potential damage from malicious or faulty scripts. A Deno cache
directory is created under that user to ensure correct permissions during runtime.
Building Applications with Coding Models | 309
Finally, you add a minimal entrypoint. The result is a lightweight, deterministic, and
secure environment that isolates model-produced code and enforces strict safety
boundaries during deployment.
Example 11-1. Set up Dockerfile
FROM python:3.11-slim
ARG DENO_VERSION=2.1.0
ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \
PIP_NO_CACHE_DIR=1 \
DENO_INSTALL=/usr/local
RUN apt-get update \
&& apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
curl ca-certificates bash git tini unzip libgfortran5 \
&& rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
RUN curl -fsSL https://deno.land/install.sh | sh -s -- -q \
&& chmod 0755 /usr/local/bin/deno \
&& /usr/local/bin/deno --version \
&& /usr/local/bin/deno run --node-modules-dir=auto -A - <<'JS'
console.log("deno OK with --node-modules-dir=auto");
JS
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir mcp-run-python==0.0.21
COPY requirements.txt /tmp/requirements.txt
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir --prefer-binary -r /tmp/requirements.txt
RUN useradd -m -u 10001 mcp
USER mcp
WORKDIR /home/mcp
ENV DENO_DIR=/home/mcp/.deno_dir
RUN mkdir -p "$DENO_DIR"
COPY --chown=mcp:mcp entrypoint.sh /usr/local/bin/entrypoint.sh
RUN chmod +x /usr/local/bin/entrypoint.sh
EXPOSE 3333
ENTRYPOINT ["/usr/bin/tini","--"]
CMD ["/usr/local/bin/entrypoint.sh","streamable-http"]
Non-root user.
You can build the image with docker build -t mcp-run-python:latest . in the
project directory.
310 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
As a next step, you need to start the MCP server (Example 11-2). This runs the
Python MCP server inside the container.
Example 11-2. Start MCP server
set -euo pipefail
MODE="${1:-streamable-http}"
PORT="${PORT:-3333}"
DEPS="${DEPS:-}"
ARGS=()
if [ -n "${DEPS}" ]; then
ARGS+=(--deps "${DEPS}")
fi
if [ "$MODE" = "stdio" ]; then
exec python -m mcp_run_python stdio "${ARGS[@]}"
elif [ "$MODE" = "streamable-http" ]; then
exec python -m mcp_run_python streamable-http --port "${PORT}" "${ARGS[@]}"
elif [ "$MODE" = "example" ]; then
exec python -m mcp_run_python ${ARGS:+--deps "${DEPS}"} example
else
echo "Unknown mode: $MODE (use stdio | streamable-http | example)"
exit 1
fi
To start the server from this script, you run simply docker run --rm -it mcp-run-
python:latest /usr/local/bin/entrypoint.sh stdio. This starts up the server,
and you can execute Python code in a safe environment.
Full Running Code
The full code example can be found in this repository.

how to create a coding sandbox with E2B.

def create_sandbox() -> Sandbox:
api_key = os.environ.get("E2B_API_KEY", "")
if not api_key:
raise RuntimeError("E2B_API_KEY is not set")
return Sandbox.create()

Example 11-4. Run a single sandbox
if __name__ == "__main__":

try:

initial_state: FibState = {
"messages": [
HumanMessage(
content="""Write an efficient Fibonacci
implementation fib(n: int) -> int."""
)
],
"iteration": 0,
"max_iterations": 3,

print("\nRationale:\n")

if isinstance(tests, TestResult):
print("\nTests passed:", tests.passed)
print("Details:", tests.details)
if tests.failed_cases:
print("Failed cases:", tests.failed_cases)
finally:
sb.kill()
Another way to safeguard the access to the rest of the system, and specifically if you
have to scale your system, is to use microVM layers such as gVisor or Firecracker
runtimes. gVisor, for instance, can easily be activated for your Kubernetes cluster on
GCP. At the end, whatever setup you’re choosing, the goal is always the same: treat
model-generated code as untrusted, and ensure that its execution can’t affect the sur‐
rounding system or other users.
312 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Evaluating LLM Deployments in Production
Another important part you want to look into is how to evaluate your LLM deploy‐
ment under real production conditions. A model that performs well in isolated tests
may still fail once it encounters concurrent traffic, long contexts, or variable work‐
loads. For this reason, you should treat deployment not as a single decision but as a
continuous process of measurement, iteration, and optimization. The goal is to deter‐
mine whether your deployment meets your latency, throughput, reliability, and cost
targets. LLM inference has several observable performance dimensions:
Time to first token (TTFT)
The user-perceived responsiveness
Inter-token latency
The rate at which the model streams output
End-to-end latency
The total time a user waits for a complete response
Throughput in tokens per second (TPS)
The measure of your serving stack’s efficiency
Capacity
The number of requests per minute the system can sustain before degrading
Error rate
The indicator of system stability under load and concurrency
All of these can be measured with a simple workload harness (“Cost Efficiency and
Hardware Comparison” on page 314) that generates realistic input and output token
distributions and submits requests at configurable concurrency levels. This allows
you to evaluate not only managed APIs (OpenAI, Anthropic, Nebius) but also your
own deployments on vLLM or SGLang. With these metrics, you can perform three
essential categories of production evaluation:
Capacity testing
Capacity testing determines how much load a deployment can sustain before vio‐
lating service-level objectives by holding the workload constant and gradually
increasing concurrency. The goal is to find the sweet spot where throughput is
maximized while latency and error rates remain stable. It tracks metrics such as
mean and p95 TTFT, mean and p95 end-to-end latency, tokens per second, com‐
pleted requests per minute, and failure patterns like timeouts or 5xx responses.
The capacity point is the highest load at which performance remains predictable;
beyond it, systems begin to queue, degrade, or fail.
Evaluating LLM Deployments in Production | 313
Stress testing
Stress testing intentionally pushes the system beyond the sweet spot to reveal how
it behaves under unsafe or extreme load. Rather than optimizing performance, it
seeks to understand failure modes, whether latency degrades gradually or collap‐
ses, whether the system can recover automatically, which error codes dominate,
and whether internal components such as the KV cache fragment become
memory-constrained. Stress tests expose architectural weaknesses in scheduling,
batching, caching, and rate limiting.
Soak testing
Soak testing evaluates whether the system can sustain the sweet spot over long
durations by running steady load for tens of minutes or hours. It uncovers slow-
burn degradation patterns such as drift in TTFT or end-to-end latency, rising
error rates, GPU memory leaks or fragmentation, increasing cache reclamation
overhead, and throttling behavior from managed APIs. Soak tests are especially
important for self-hosted deployments and GPU-backed microservices, where
fragmentation and resource pressure accumulate over time.
Cost Efficiency and Hardware Comparison
Performance evaluation is only one dimension of deploying transformer models. In
practice, every deployment decision is coupled to a cost model: the price of managed
APIs, the hourly cost of GPU hardware, and the efficiency of the underlying runtime.
The following benchmarking framework is from Ray, a Python-native framework for
distributed computing, slightly adapted for later Python versions, so I’d recommend
to use the version from my repo.
To illustrate how you can use this framework, let’s evaluate two variants of the
Qwen3-32B model provided through the Nebius managed API: the fast tier and the
base tier. Both are benchmarked under identical workloads, allowing direct compari‐
son of latency, throughput, and error behavior. You’ll find a notebook with the com‐
plete code in the book’s repo.
Example 11-5 shows how to initiate a benchmark run for the Qwen3-32B-fast API
endpoint.
Example 11-5. Run benchmark against the Qwen3-32B-fast API endpoint
%%bash
export OPENAI_API_KEY="${NEBIUS_API_KEY}"
export OPENAI_API_BASE="https://api.tokenfactory.nebius.com/v1"
python token_benchmark_ray.py \
--model "Qwen/Qwen3-32B-fast" \
--mean-input-tokens 1024 \
--stddev-input-tokens 200 \

"temperature": 0.0,

These results show a clear difference between the two service tiers: the fast variant
more than doubles throughput and significantly reduces inter-token latency while
keeping TTFT nearly identical. Such measurements form the basis for cost-efficient
model selection, helping you compare API tiers, GPU types, quantization strategies,
or self-hosted deployments using the same workload and the same evaluation
harness.
Production Gotcha: The “Avg Latency” Lie
Never optimize solely for average latency (mean). In production,
your user’s experience is defined by the tail latency (p95 or p99). A
system with a low mean but high p99 can feel “broken” to 1 in 20
users. Always run your capacity tests until the p95 metric violates
your service level objective (SLO), the specific latency target you
promised your users (such as 500 ms max per task), rather than
just watching the average.
The numerical comparison in Table 11-3 is informative, but the performance gap
becomes even clearer when visualized. Figure 11-2 plots mean output throughput
and mean end-to-end latency for both service tiers. Although the two variants have
nearly identical TTFT, their streaming performance differs dramatically.

Figure 11-2. Throughput and latency comparison between Qwen3-32B fast and
base tiers.
The fast tier achieves more than 2.6× higher throughput and reduces end-to-end
latency from 6.48 seconds to 1.39 seconds, a difference users will perceive immedi‐
ately. This illustrates that the performance characteristics of a deployment are shaped
not only by the model architecture but also by the serving stack, batching strategy,
and hardware tier behind the API.
To understand how these latency differences translate into practical capacity,

workload and concurrency settings.

Here, the difference becomes operationally significant. The fast tier sustains almost
69 requests per minute, compared to 26 requests per minute for the base tier. In a
production environment, this gap directly affects how many concurrent users can be
supported, how quickly a system recovers from bursts of traffic, and ultimately how
much the deployment costs at scale.
Calculating GenAI Prices
GenAI Prices is a library by Pydantic that provides a compact way to compute infer‐
ence costs across providers. It resolves model and provider identifiers through an
internal matching engine, applies historic and time varying price schedules, and han‐
dles more complex structures such as DeepSeek’s off-peak pricing or the tiered con‐
text billing used by Gemini models. The library can also identify discrepancies
between vendor documentation and observed prices. It’s available as both a Python
and JavaScript package with a simple CLI.
Taken together, these two visualizations illustrate an important lesson: model perfor‐
mance is inseparable from infrastructure performance. A model that appears identi‐
cal on paper can behave very differently depending on the inference tier, hardware
class, and runtime implementation used by the provider. Evaluating these factors is
essential for choosing the right balance between speed, scalability, and cost.

Quantization
This section doesn’t aim to explain different quantization methods but to give you a
way of validating which adapter strategy might be the best choice. The following code
will compare LoRA,2 QLoRA,3 DoRA,4 and LoftQ5 on a single base model
(Qwen3-4B-Base) and a single domain (step-by-step math reasoning) and then vary
only the adapter strategy. Each configuration trains on the same GPU, supervised
fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning setup, so any difference in behavior comes
from the adapter and quantization choice. The supervised fine-tuning phase uses a
chain-of-thought–style dataset to teach the model to separate its working from its
final answer with explicit tags. The GRPO phase then applies a simple reward func‐
tion that checks both the output format and the correctness of the final solution. The
training loop records three signals that matter for deployment:
2 Edward J. Hu et al. “LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models” (2021).
3 Tim Dettmers et al. “QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs” (2023).
4 Shih-Yang Liu et al. “DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation” (2024).
5 Yixiao Li et al. “LoftQ: LoRA-Fine-Tuning-Aware Quantization for Large Language Models” (2023).
320 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
• How quickly each adapter converges during supervised fine-tuning, through the
Cross-Entropy Loss curve
• How well each setup learns the reward in the GRPO phase, through the reward
trajectory
• How fast the adapted model can generate tokens during inference, measured as
tokens per second for a simple prompt
Before you test the individual methods, it helps to understand how the different
adapter families approach parameter-efficient fine-tuning. Each technique modifies
only a small portion of the model, but they differ in how they introduce low-rank
structure, how they interact with quantization, and how much expressive power they
recover relative to full fine-tuning. The following summaries highlight the core idea
behind each adapter strategy and what makes it distinct:
LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation
LoRA injects a small pair of low-rank matrices into selected linear layers and
updates only those matrices during training. The base weights remain untouched
and stay in full precision. This is the classic parameter-efficient baseline: stable,
simple, and a good reference point for all other methods.
QLoRA: Quantized LoRA
QLoRA loads the base model directly in four-bit precision and trains LoRA
adapters on top of those quantized weights. Only the adapters get updated, but
gradients still flow “through” the quantized weights. This isolates the effect of
quantization: you keep the LoRA structure unchanged while drastically reducing
memory usage.
DoRA: Direction and Magnitude Decomposition
DoRA follows the same low-rank philosophy as LoRA but decomposes each tar‐
get weight into a magnitude and a direction. The low-rank update adjusts the
direction, while a small scalar pathway adjusts the magnitude. This mimics more
closely full fine-tuning without increasing inference cost, often giving the adapter
more expressive power under the same parameter budget.
Quantization | 321
LoftQ: Quantization-Aware LoRA Initialization
LoftQ couples quantization and adapter initialization. Instead of quantizing the
model first and then attaching randomly initialized adapters, it computes quan‐
tized weights and a matching low-rank initialization jointly. This “builds in”
quantization effects, so the adapters begin aligned with the model you actually
train. The result is LoRA on a quantized base but with an initialization that
wastes less capacity counteracting quantization error.
Before running the benchmark, you need a minimal configuration that fixes the base
model, dataset size, and training schedules for both supervised fine-tuning and
GRPO. Example 11-7 defines these constants so that every adapter variant is evalu‐
ated under identical conditions.
Example 11-7. Setup for the quantization benchmark
MODEL_ID = "unsloth/Qwen3-4B-Base"
SFT_STEPS = 60
GRPO_STEPS = 200
GRPO_GENERATIONS = 4
DATASET_SIZE = 500
Controls the single base model used for all experiments.
Defines how long you train in each phase. Higher values produce smoother
curves and more stable rewards, at the cost of more compute.
Specifies how many sampled completions you use per update step in GRPO,
which reduces variance in the reward signal.
Caps how many math problems you use from each dataset, so you can scale the
benchmark up or down to fit your hardware budget.
Use an adjusted, smaller dataset size.
With the global configuration in place, the next step is to specify the adapter variants
and wrap them in a simple loop that trains, evaluates, and logs their performance.
Example 11-8 allows the benchmark to cycle through LoRA, QLoRA, DoRA, and
LoftQ using the same data, same training phases, and the same hardware budget.
322 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Example 11-8. Adapter configurations and benchmark loop
ADAPTERS = {
"LoRA": {"4bit": False,
"peft": {"r": 16, "lora_alpha": 16,
"target_modules": ["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj"]}},
"QLoRA": {"4bit": True,
"peft": {"r": 16, "lora_alpha": 16,
"target_modules": ["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj"]}},
"DoRA": {"4bit": False,
"peft": {"r": 16, "lora_alpha": 16, "use_dora": True,
"target_modules": ["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj"]}},
"LoftQ": {"4bit": False,
"peft": {"r": 16, "lora_alpha": 16,
"target_modules": ["q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj", "o_proj"],
"init_lora_weights": "loftq",
"loftq_config": LoftQConfig(loftq_bits=4, loftq_iter=1)}}
}
Each entry in ADAPTERS describes one strategy, with two pieces of information:
whether the base model is loaded in four-bit mode (“4bit”) and the parameter-
efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) configuration passed into FastLanguageModel.get_
peft_model. LoRA and QLoRA share the same low-rank structure and target mod‐
ules. QLoRA differs only by enabling four-bit loading, which isolates the effect of
quantizing the base weights. DoRA adds use_dora=True, which decomposes the
weight updates, so you can see whether this extra flexibility buys you better loss or
reward under the same training budget. LoftQ uses the same LoRA shape but initial‐
izes adapter weights with LoftQ via init_lora_weights="loftq" and a LoftQConfig.
This lets you test different quantization adapters without changing the rest of the
pipeline.
DenseMixer to Improve MoE Training
DenseMixer gives MoE models more accurate router gradients
without requiring changes to your training code. It works with any
Top-k MoE trained via backpropagation and can be enabled as a
plug-and-play addition. DenseMixer integrates with common
training stacks (transformers, llama-factory, open-instruct, verl)
and is compatible with parameter-efficient approaches such as
LoRA.
Quantization | 323
This setup will run for about 5 hours on an A100 GPU. The following charts are
from this training, and they tell you a very clear story about the trade-offs for differ‐
ent adapters. Figure 11-4 shows the results from the supervised fine-tuning with dif‐
ferent adapters.
Figure 11-4. Comparison of results from supervised fine-tuning with different adapters.
DoRA (dotted line) is the “best student”: the dotted line is the deepest from all four.
Since DoRA separates the magnitude and direction of weights, this allows it to learn
the syntax more efficiently. The “worst student” is QLoRA (dashed line). This is due
to the quantization tax, because the model is struggling to learn precise formatting
through the noise of 4-bit quantization.
LoRAX for Large-Scale Multi-Adapter Serving
LoRAX is a multi-LoRA inference server that can host thousands
of fine-tuned variants on a single GPU. It uses dynamic adapter
loading to keep memory usage low while preserving throughput
and latency, making it a cost-effective solution for serving large
fleets of personalized or domain-specialized models.
324 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
If you now look at the plot from the GRPO training (Figure 11-5), you see that LoftQ
(dash-dot) spikes up to a reward of 0.05 around step 75.
Figure 11-5. Comparison of results from GRPO training with different adapters.
The reason why LoftQ performs better is that it initializes the weights intelligently—
that is, canceling out quantization error. Therefore, the model is still “smart” enough
to stumble upon the correct answer/reasoning much earlier than the others. LoRA
(solid line) finally found a reward around step 190, while QLoRA and DoRA need
400+ steps to start learning reasoning strategies.
Figure 11-6 shows the inference speed comparison across the different adapters.
Quantization | 325
Figure 11-6. Inference speed comparison across the different adapters.
The standard 4-bit QLoRA introduces an ~17% latency penalty due to the on-the-fly
dequantization (unpacking 4-bit to 16-bit during generation), while LoftQ is almost
as fast as LoRA (because it merges well) but uses the low VRAM of QLoRA. Given
this data, LoftQ would be a reasonable choice for deployment, as it bridges the gap: it
fits in memory (4-bit), infers quickly (15.9 TPS), and initializes “smart enough” to
find the reasoning rewards early.
Test-Time Low-Rank Adaptation in
Vision-Language Models
So far, I’ve treated deployment in this chapter as a mostly static decision. You pick a
model, possibly fine-tune it, quantize it, then put it behind an inference server. In
practice, many real-world systems must cope with slow domain drift. Image distribu‐
tions change, lighting conditions shift, camera hardware is upgraded, and the test dis‐
tribution no longer matches the data the model was originally trained on.
One way to react to this is periodic fine-tuning with new labeled data. A more agile
alternative is test-time adaptation. In this setting, you allow a tiny number of parame‐
ters to adjust at inference time, driven by a self-supervised objective on the incoming
test samples themselves.
326 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Test Time Low Rank Adaptation (TTL)6 allows this dynamic adaption for Contrastive
Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP)-style encoders. Instead of tuning prompts, TTL
inserts low-rank adapters into the attention layers of the visual encoder and updates
only those adapters during test-time, while the rest of CLIP stays frozen.
The adaptation signal comes from confidence rather than labels. For each test image,
the system creates several augmented views and passes them through CLIP with the
usual class text prompts. TTL then applies a weighted entropy loss over the predicted
class probabilities for those augmentations. The objective encourages consistent,
high-confidence predictions across all views. The gradient updates only the low-rank
adapters in the visual encoder. Prompts and backbone weights remain unchanged.
From a deployment perspective, TTL is interesting because:
• It respects strict zero-shot assumptions. No target domain labels and no access to
the original training data are required.
• The number of trainable parameters is a tiny fraction of the full model, which
makes adaptation cheap and fast.
• Updates can be performed in a single optimization step per test sample or per
small batch of samples.
Modality-Grounded Audio Reasoning
Step-Audio-R1 is an audio reasoning model that can be used with
test-time compute scaling. The model overcomes the textual surro‐
gate reasoning problem, where models initialized from text rely on
transcript-style abstractions instead of acoustic cues. The model is
available via Hugging Face.
The important point isn’t the exact loss formulation but the pattern. You can treat
adapters as a deployment time control surface that lets you track domain shift
without ever touching the backbone. You could apply a similar pattern to other
modalities, such as in audio classification. Here, you could adapt low-rank blocks in
the spectrogram encoder using a consistency objective across augmentations, such as
time masking or frequency masking.
6 Raza Imam et al. “Test-Time Low Rank Adaptation via Confidence Maximization for Zero-Shot
Generalization of Vision-Language Models” (2024).
Test-Time Low-Rank Adaptation in Vision-Language Models | 327
The backbone remains a stable, pretrained foundation model, but your deployment
now becomes a composition of three elements:
• A frozen foundation model
• A small, trainable adapter layer
• A self-supervised test-time objective that nudges the adapter toward the current
input distribution
When you design a production system for non-text modalities, it’s worth deciding
explicitly whether the model will remain static in deployment or whether you want to
use this kind of dynamic adaptation. The latter introduces new operational overhead
around monitoring, rollback, and stability, but it can significantly improve robustness
without the cost of full retraining.
Conclusion
In this chapter, you saw that deployment is less about wiring up an endpoint and
more about making structural choices. The spectrum from closed APIs to managed
open models to self-hosted deployments determines who controls cost, data, observa‐
bility, and long-term independence. Understanding the architecture family you serve,
from decoder-only LLMs to encoder-only perception models and encoder-decoder or
diffusion stacks, lets you align deployment strategy with the runtime, memory, and
concurrency behavior of the model itself.
You learned that most headaches in practice come from the inference runtime, not
from the checkpoint. Decoder-only models live and die by KV cache behavior, batch‐
ing, and scheduling, which is why mechanisms such as paged attention, prefix reuse,
and continuous batching matter as much as the underlying model weights. At the
same time, distributed inference is insecure by default. Secure deployments treat
intra-cluster traffic, media access, and machine-level permissions as untrusted and
rely on isolation, explicit configuration, and least privilege to keep powerful models
from becoming powerful attack surfaces.
You also saw how deployment changes once models can execute code. Coding models
behave like agents with system capabilities, so safe deployment begins with strict
sandboxing. MCP-based containers, E2B-style sandboxes, and microVM layers such
as gVisor or Firecracker provide the isolation needed to run generated code without
risking the rest of your environment. On the evaluation side, capacity, stress, and soak
tests turn LLM serving into an empirical process, while benchmarking frameworks
and tools such as GenAI Prices let you compare providers, hardware tiers, and run‐
times in terms of both latency and cost per tokens.
328 | Chapter 11: Deploying Transformer Models
Finally, you explored quantization and adaptation through the lens of deployment
trade-offs. You saw how LoRA, QLoRA, DoRA, and LoftQ differ in convergence,
reward learning, and inference speed, with LoftQ offering an attractive balance
between VRAM usage and runtime performance. Test-time low-rank adaptation
extends this idea into production, using tiny adapter surfaces and self-supervised
objectives to track slow domain shift without retraining the backbone.
With these principles and tools, you’re ready to think about deployment as its own
design space. Chapter 12 takes a look at where to go next and where the field is
heading.
Conclusion | 329
CHAPTER 12
Where to Go Next: From Models
to Intelligent Systems
This is the last chapter of the book, and by now you’ve learned how diverse and
adaptable the transformer architecture is. You started from first principles and saw
how a single architecture can power models for time series, vision, image and video
generation, audio, reinforcement learning, planning, and coding. You also saw how
test-time compute, such as tree search or reranking, and reinforcement learning can
enhance performance and steer behavior. And you saw how agentic systems create
loops of planning, action, feedback, and self-correction.
This was never meant to be a collection of isolated techniques. It was meant to show
that transformers have become a general interface between data, decisions, and tools.
Treat this book as your foundation to move from individual models to larger systems
of specialist components working together to solve real tasks. By now, you under‐
stand the architecture, the patterns, and the reasoning that make transformers work
across domains. Your next step is not learning another model or chasing another
benchmark. The next step is learning how to wire these components into something
coherent, reliable, maintainable, and capable of improvement through experience.
The shift happening in the field reflects this. Progress is no longer defined by a single
checkpoint or a bigger context window. It’s defined by systems that integrate multiple
models, allocate compute at test-time, coordinate tool usage, manage memory, and
learn from their own errors. Table 12-1 shows a snapshot of the number of papers
published in 2024 and 2025 across these domains.
331
Table 12-1. rLLM components in the RL training loop (2024 versus 2025)
Topic 2024 papers 2025 papers
Agentic and multi-agent systems 820 2,520

Self-improving systems plus transformers or LLMs 209 458
Test-time compute plus transformers or LLMs 395 1,053
The table shows that this direction isn’t theoretical. It’s visible in the research distribu‐
tion itself. I scraped arXiv for 2024 and 2025 papers across the domains shown in the
table. The scrape is not exhaustive, since some papers will fall through the cracks due
to naming conventions, missing keywords, or uploads outside the query window. The
spikes around May/June and November are to be expected, as this is influenced by
ICML and NeurIPS submission cycles, which naturally increase volume around those
months. Even with that context, the trend is clear. The jump from 2024 to 2025 for
agentic/multi-agent systems suggests that these systems are the primary focus of the
world’s top research labs. The rise in test-time compute (increasing by nearly 3×)
indicates that the industry has realized it’s more efficient to let a model think longer
(search, reason, verify) than it is to just make the model bigger. The field is not scal‐
ing parameter count; it’s scaling coordination, alignment, and improvement across
components. Models are no longer endpoints. They are parts of systems. Figures 12-1
and 12-2 illustrate the flow and increase over the two years.
Figure 12-1. Research momentum in 2024: from RL to agentic and self-improving
systems.

Figure 12-2. Research momentum in 2025: from RL to agentic and self-improving

This is why my intent for this last chapter is to transition your thinking from a
model-focused practitioner to a system thinker. Someone who can connect multiple
models with intention. Someone who understands that each model in a system intro‐
duces strengths and weaknesses. Someone who recognizes that the system is only as
reliable as the weakest point in its design. This is how it all connects; the fundamen‐
tals you learned in this book matter. They are the difference between building a frag‐
ile demo and building an intelligent system you can rely on in production. Recent
research reflects this shift, and the next section demonstrates how.
Combining Capabilities: SAM 3 Agent
Performance is no longer the product of a stronger model alone but of the interaction
between model capability, coordination structure, and task properties. A clear exam‐
ple appears in the research published alongside SAM 3. The team combined a multi‐
modal large language model (MLLM) with SAM 31 as a tool and let the system iterate:
propose a query, call SAM 3, inspect the masks, refine, and repeat until the instruc‐
tion is satisfied. The improvement does not come from stronger vision or stronger
language. It comes from the loop that connects them. The gain is architectural, not
parametric.
1 Nicolas Carion et al. “SAM 3: Segment Anything with Concepts” (2025).
Combining Capabilities: SAM 3 Agent | 333
In Chapter 3, you saw how SAM 3 can segment visual content, but the workflow
remained manual. Even with SAM 3 (“Segment Videos and Images with Concept
Prompts” on page 88), where text prompts can guide segmentation, the model still
acts alone. It waits for input, executes a single task, and returns a mask. It’s a capable
component, but it’s not a system yet. To answer more complex segmentation prompts,
such as “the leftmost red yarn ball,” when applied to an input image (Figure 12-3),
SAM 3 must be connected to an MLLM in an agent loop.
Figure 12-3. Example image for more difficult text query.
In this setup, segmentation is no longer a single request. It becomes a step in a plan.
The MLLM interprets the query, proposes an action, and generates a tool call. SAM 3
executes the segmentation task as a specialist. The agent evaluates the result, selects
the correct mask, and decides whether to continue or stop. The workflow becomes
iterative, reflective, and goal-oriented. Figures 12-4 through 12-6 visualize this
workflow.
334 | Chapter 12: Where to Go Next: From Models to Intelligent Systems
Figure 12-4. Bird’s-eye view of workflow.
Once the user query is received, the agent coordinator (AC) initializes the session. It
acts as the central hub, keeping track of the segmentation process. From here, the
state is passed to the MLLM planner, which enters a cognitive loop to decide how to
handle the request. Here’s a step-by-step breakdown:
1. Analyze: the model looks at the current visual state and any previous feedback.
2. Think: it breaks the user’s request into logical subtasks.
3. Remind: it reviews system constraints (such as “Don’t overwrite specific masks”).
4. Plan: it selects the most efficient path forward.
5. Decide: it commits to a specific tool call.
Figure 12-5 illustrates this decision process.
Combining Capabilities: SAM 3 Agent | 335
Figure 12-5. Cognitive loop of the system.
After the MLLM makes a decision, it exits the loop to execute a tool. This leads to a
branching path (Figure 12-6): either we need more information or the loop can
terminate.
Figure 12-6. This stage separates intermediate tools (which help the agent “see” better)
from Terminal Tools (which end the session).
336 | Chapter 12: Where to Go Next: From Models to Intelligent Systems
This is the systems-level moment of the book I want to leave you with as an overview
of where to go next or how to think about your next AI project. An MLLM alone
can’t isolate the correct object. SAM 3 alone can’t interpret the instruction. The agent
alone can’t ground the decision. The result appears only when different models coop‐
erate. The system validates, selects, and continues the loop until the goal is reached.
This is orchestration. This is the shift from operating models to designing systems.
Using SAM 3 in an agentic setup is fairly straightforward. As usual, you first load the
model and processor. Example 12-1 shows how to do this.
Example 12-1. Load SAM 3 model and processor
bpe_path = "/content/sam3/sam3/assets/bpe_simple_vocab_16e6.txt.gz"
model = build_sam3_image_model(bpe_path=bpe_path)
processor = Sam3Processor(model, confidence_threshold=0.5)
The code from the SAM 3 repo works with OpenAI-compatible APIs, so you can just
start an vLLM server while allowing local media. Example 12-2 illustrates how to do
this.
Example 12-2. Start vLLM server in background
nohup vllm serve Qwen/Qwen3-VL-8B-Thinking \
--served-model-name qwen3_vl_8b_thinking \
--enforce-eager \
--port 8001 \
--host 0.0.0.0 \
--allowed-local-media-path / \
> vllm_server.log 2>&1 &
Security Risk with Media Path
The --allowed-local-media-path / flag grants the model server
read access to your entire root file system. While this is convenient
for local development and Google Colab environments, it’s a signif‐
icant security risk in a production environment. If a malicious
actor gains access to your agent loop, they could use the MLLM to
“inspect” and exfiltrate sensitive files (like .env files or SSH keys)
under the guise of image processing. Always restrict this path to a
specific, isolated directory (such as --allowed-local-media-
path /home/user/app/data), and run your agent in a container‐
ized environment.
Combining Capabilities: SAM 3 Agent | 337
Example 12-3 shows how to set up the model configurations correctly, as it’s impor‐
tant that the server model name and the server URL match precisely.
Example 12-3. Simple text generation
LLM_CONFIGS = {
"qwen3_vl_8b_thinking": {
"provider": "vllm",
"model": "qwen3_vl_8b_thinking",
},
}
model = "qwen3_vl_8b_thinking"
LLM_API_KEY = "DUMMY_API_KEY"
llm_config = LLM_CONFIGS[model]
llm_config["api_key"] = LLM_API_KEY
llm_config["name"] = model
if llm_config["provider"] == "vllm":
LLM_SERVER_URL = "http://127.0.0.1:8001/v1"
else:
LLM_SERVER_URL = llm_config["base_url"]
Now you can just load your image, in my case the cute kitten surrounded by
different-colored yarn balls. To combine the MLLM and SAM 3, you can use the
agent functions provided by the SAM 3 team. Example 12-4 shows how to handle
more complex prompts.
Example 12-4. Agent inference with SAM 3 and Qwen3 VL
prompt = "the leftmost red ball"
image = os.path.abspath(image)
send_generate_request = partial(send_generate_request_orig,
server_url=LLM_SERVER_URL,
model=llm_config["model"],
api_key=llm_config["api_key"])
call_sam_service = partial(call_sam_service_orig, sam3_processor=processor)
output_image_path = run_single_image_inference(
image, prompt, llm_config, send_generate_request, call_sam_service,
debug=True, output_dir="agent_output"
)
Example 12-5 shows how you can display the image with the segmentation mask.
Example 12-5. Display image with segmentation mask
if output_image_path is not None:
display(Image(filename=output_image_path))
338 | Chapter 12: Where to Go Next: From Models to Intelligent Systems
Figure 12-7 displays the final image with the segmentation mask and the number 1.
Figure 12-7. Image with segmentation mask.
What you’ve just seen here, by combining a segmentation model with an MLLM in
an agentic system, is exactly what you’ll encounter in multi-agent systems as well.
That is, once a single model baseline crosses a certain threshold, adding more agents
no longer guarantees improvement. Coordination overhead, fragmented context, and
error propagation can erase the advantage of scale. In other words, the limitation isn’t
architectural size but rather architectural alignment. These systems behave like
organisms, not collections of isolated parts, and they require design decisions that
treat them as such.
The Science of Scaling Agentic Systems
As you’ve seen in the previous section, you can’t just “prompt your way out” of
system-level failure. In fact, in a comment on LinkedIn, I was asked, “Is it really
worth investing time to learn prompt engineering techniques? Does a very well craf‐
ted prompt lead to meaningfully better responses from an LLM than a reasonably
good one?” This section here is my long answer to that, as I believe the future lies in
the combination of systems and how they evolve over time.
The Science of Scaling Agentic Systems | 339
This is where recent research on system-level scaling becomes useful, because it gives
you a vocabulary to reason about why some systems improve and others collapse. In
a paper2 published by Google Research and Google DeepMind, the authors showed
that the difference between a system that gets better with every loop and one that falls
apart isn’t the number of agents or the size of the model. It’s how the system is wired.
The coordination structure, the flow of information, and the topology of decision
making determine whether the system amplifies capability or amplifies error. In other
words: performance is an architectural outcome.
If an agentic system loops, stalls, or amplifies its own errors, it’s often not a prompt or
fine-tuning issue. It’s a topology issue. Some tasks benefit from centralized coordina‐
tion, where a supervisor validates and routes decisions to prevent cascading mistakes.
Others benefit from decentralized patterns, where specialists explore in parallel and
exchange information peer-to-peer. Some tasks will break down in multi-agent set‐
tings, because sequential reasoning with strict dependencies is often more stable
when handled by a single agent supported by tools. System design becomes the opti‐
mization step, not parameter count.
Taking the Next Step
If you want to deepen the system-level mindset and explore build‐
ing AI agents more deeply, I go further into this system-level think‐
ing and skill set approach in my book AI Agents: The Definitive
Guide (O’Reilly).
This aligns with the early principles of collaborative scaling.3 The process of increas‐
ing the number of agents doesn’t behave like the process of increasing parameter
count in a transformer. Neural scaling4 is continuous; collaborative scaling is condi‐
tional. It grows, plateaus, and collapses depending on communication costs, memory
constraints, and how much context each agent actually sees. This is why topologies
matter: chains, trees, and different designs behave differently under load.
As you move toward production, system planning becomes essential. When I design
AI systems or agentic systems, I think of the models, or agents, as dynamic, collabo‐
rative systems rather than isolated components. Every model size and model family
has a distinct skill profile. If you build your system with that in mind, you’ll not only
get better results; you’ll also save money.
2 Yubin Kim et al. “Towards a Science of Scaling Agent Systems” (2025).
3 Chen Qian et al. “Scaling Large Language Model-Based Multi-Agent Collaboration” (2025).
4 Jared Kaplan et al. “Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models” (2020).
340 | Chapter 12: Where to Go Next: From Models to Intelligent Systems
Conclusion
In this final chapter, you learned that the real frontier is no longer a single model. It’s
the space between models. The moment you connect components, you move from
isolated capability to coordinated behavior, and every design choice begins to matter.
Intelligence is becoming modular. Architecture becomes intent. Topology becomes a
lever. Tooling becomes part of the cognitive loop rather than an accessory attached to
the side.
You now understand that system performance doesn’t emerge from scale alone. It
comes from structure. Supervisor patterns, centralized planners, peer-to-peer special‐
ists, and single-agent loops with tools all behave differently under pressure. Some
amplify strengths. Some amplify failure. Coordination shapes outcome more than
parameter count. The wiring determines whether a system stabilizes, improves, or
collapses.
With that, you’ve reached the point where you see that thinking like a model practi‐
tioner is no longer enough. Thinking like a systems designer is the next step. Some‐
one who treats architecture, memory, interaction, and evaluation as part of a system.
Someone who understands that capability is not only in the model but in the space
between models.
This Definitive Guide ends here, but your work in the era of experience and systems
design is just beginning.

About the Author
Nicole Koenigstein is an AI researcher and practitioner in agentic systems, working
across research, consulting, teaching, and direct system implementation to build reli‐
able, production-ready AI systems. Her work focuses on multi-agent architectures,
evaluation, safety, and long-term system behavior.
She served as an external evaluator for a European Commission AI Grand Challenge
and has advised IOSCO on generative AI in regulated environments. She also serves
on advisory boards for leading AI and quantitative finance conferences. Nicole regu‐
larly delivers invited talks and technical workshops across academia, industry, and
international events.
She is the author of Math for Machine Learning and Transformers in Action with Man‐
ning Publications. Her forthcoming books, Transformers: The Definitive Guide—
Applications Beyond NLP and AI Agents: The Definitive Guide, will be published by
O’Reilly Media.
Colophon
The animal on the cover of Transformers: The Definitive Guide is a wattled jacana
(Jacana jacana).
The wattled jacana is a tropical bird known for its remarkable ability to walk on float‐
ing vegetation, thanks to its long toes and claws that distribute its weight over lily
pads and other aquatic plants. Native to Central and South America, this bird inhab‐
its freshwater wetlands, marshes, and ponds, where it forages in slow-moving water.
The wattled jacana is recognized by its chestnut-brown body, black head and neck,
and yellow flight feathers. It sports a red frontal shield and wattle on the face, which
give the species its name. The bird can live up to seven years in the wild.
According to the IUCN Red List, the wattled jacana’s conservation status is of Least
Concern. Its adaptability to various wetland habitats and its widespread distribution
contribute to its secure conservation status, although wetland drainage and habitat
degradation can pose localized threats.
Many of the animals on O’Reilly covers are endangered; all of them are important to
the world.
The cover illustration is by José Marzan Jr., based on a black-and-white engraving
from Riverside Natural History. The series design is by Edie Freedman, Ellie
Volckhausen, and Karen Montgomery. The cover fonts are Gilroy Semibold and
Guardian Sans. The text font is Adobe Minion Pro; the heading font is Adobe Myriad
Condensed; and the code font is Dalton Maag’s Ubuntu Mono.
